# 1D — not separable:   ● ○○ ●
# Map to 2D (x → x²):  ●        ●
#                          ○○
# Now a straight line separates them

The kernel trick: you never compute the transformation φ(x) directly. Instead, the kernel function K(x, x') = φ(x)·φ(x') computes the dot product in the high-dimensional space using only the original features. This is computationally cheap but gives the power of a high-dimensional separation.

Common Kernels

KernelFormulaBoundary shapeUse when
LinearK(x,x') = x·x'Straight hyperplaneText data, high-dim features, large datasets
RBF (Gaussian)K(x,x') = exp(−γ‖x−x'‖²)Flexible curvedGeneral purpose, default choice for non-linear data
PolynomialK(x,x') = (x·x' + r)^dPolynomial curvesWhen feature interactions matter

Gamma (γ) — RBF Influence Radius

γ controls how far each support vector's influence reaches in the RBF kernel:

γ valueInfluence radiusBoundaryRisk
Small γWide — affects far-away pointsSmooth, simpleUnderfitting
Large γNarrow — only affects nearby pointsWiggly, complexOverfitting

High γ makes each support vector act like a spike — the boundary wraps tightly around training points. Low γ makes each support vector cast a wide net — the boundary is smooth and general. Always tune C and γ together. Use gamma='scale' as a safe default (sklearn sets γ = 1 / (n_features × variance)).

SVC vs LinearSVC

SVC(kernel='linear')LinearSVC
AlgorithmKernel-based quadratic optimisationDirect linear optimisation
SpeedSlow O(n²–n³)Fast O(n) — scales to large data
Probability outputYes with probability=TrueNo
Use whenSmall/medium datasets, non-linear kernelsLarge datasets, text classification
Rule: If your dataset has >100k rows and linear separation is reasonable, use LinearSVC. Otherwise start with SVC(kernel='rbf').

The Scaling Requirement

SVM computes distances between points (‖x − x'‖). If one feature has values in [0, 1000] and another in [0, 1], the first dominates every distance calculation and the second becomes invisible. Always scale before SVM — wrap it in a Pipeline so scaling is never accidentally skipped.

Full sklearn Code

from sklearn.svm import SVC
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split, GridSearchCV
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report

# Step 1: Always scale inside a Pipeline — prevents data leakage
pipeline = Pipeline([
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),
    ('svm',    SVC(kernel='rbf', probability=True))
])

# Step 2: Tune C and gamma together — they interact
param_grid = {
    'svm__C':     [0.1, 1, 10, 100],
    'svm__gamma': ['scale', 0.001, 0.01, 0.1]
}
grid = GridSearchCV(pipeline, param_grid, cv=5, scoring='f1', n_jobs=-1)
grid.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Step 3: Evaluate on held-out test set
best = grid.best_estimator_
print(classification_report(y_test, best.predict(X_test)))
print("Best params:", grid.best_params_)
# For large datasets — use LinearSVC instead
from sklearn.svm import LinearSVC

pipeline_linear = Pipeline([
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),
    ('svm',    LinearSVC(C=1.0, max_iter=2000))
])
pipeline_linear.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(pipeline_linear.score(X_test, y_test))

Key Rules Card

SVM in practice — 5 rules
  1. Always scale — margin is distance-based; unscaled features destroy it
  2. Use a Pipeline — scaler must be fit on training data only
  3. Tune C and γ together — they interact; grid search both simultaneously
  4. Start with RBF — if that fails try linear; only try polynomial for specific reasons
  5. Switch to LinearSVC at scale — SVC is O(n²–n³), brutal on large datasets

🧪 Quick Check

🔄 Model Validation

Cross-validation, GridSearchCV, and Pipelines — how to get an honest estimate of model performance.

Why Validation Matters

You build a model and measure its accuracy. That number only means something if it was measured on data the model never saw during training. The more your model (or your tuning) interacts with the test set, the more optimistic your results become — and the less trustworthy they are on truly unseen data.

Bias and Variance — The Fundamental Tradeoff

Every model's prediction error has three components:

Total Error = Bias² + Variance + Irreducible Noise

Irreducible noise is random noise in the data — you cannot fix it. Focus on Bias and Variance.

Bias (Underfitting)Variance (Overfitting)
What it isModel too simple — misses the real patternModel too complex — memorises noise
SymptomPoor training AND test accuracyHigh training accuracy, much lower test accuracy
FixMore complex model, more featuresRegularisation, pruning, more data, CV tuning
ExampleDecision tree max_depth=1Decision tree max_depth=None

Three-Way Data Split

For serious work, split your data three ways — not two. The test set is a vault you open exactly once at the very end.

# Full Dataset
# ├── Training set   (70–90%) → model learns from this
# ├── Validation set  (5–15%) → tune hyperparameters here (open repeatedly)
# └── Test set        (5–15%) → final honest evaluation — touch ONCE

from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

# Step 1: carve off 20% as the held-out test set
x_train, x_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(x, y, train_size=0.8)

# Step 2: split that 20% in half → 10% validation, 10% test
x_val, x_test, y_val, y_test = train_test_split(x_test, y_test, test_size=0.5)

print(len(x_train), len(x_val), len(x_test))  # e.g. 3681, 460, 460

The discipline: Every time you tune a hyperparameter based on test performance, you corrupt that number. The validation set is your proxy test set — use it for all tuning. The real test set is seen only at the end to report your final result.

Checking Class Balance Across Splits

Always verify that your class ratio is preserved across splits — especially important for imbalanced data like spam detection.

# value_counts(normalize=True) shows proportions instead of raw counts
print(f"% spam in all data:        {data['class'].sum()/len(data):.3f}")
print(f"% spam in training data:   {y_train.sum()/len(y_train):.3f}")
print(f"% spam in validation data: {y_val.sum()/len(y_val):.3f}")
print(f"% spam in test data:       {y_test.sum()/len(y_test):.3f}")

# Or use value_counts for non-binary targets
print(y_train.value_counts(normalize=True))

What you're checking: If 39% of the full dataset is spam, each split should also be roughly 39% spam. A random split can accidentally put most rare examples in one set. Use stratify=y in train_test_split to guarantee this.

Data Leakage in Preprocessing

The most common mistake: fitting a scaler on the full dataset before splitting. The scaler learns the mean and std of test rows — those rows then influenced the transformation applied to them. The test set is no longer truly unseen.

# ❌ WRONG — scaler has seen test data (data leakage)
scaler = StandardScaler()
X_scaled = scaler.fit_transform(X)          # test rows included in mean/std
X_train, X_test = train_test_split(X_scaled)

# ✅ CORRECT — scaler only sees training data
X_train, X_test = train_test_split(X)
scaler = StandardScaler()
X_train = scaler.fit_transform(X_train)     # fit on train only
X_test  = scaler.transform(X_test)          # apply same transform, no refit
⚠️ The same rule applies to any preprocessing that learns from data: imputation (mean/median), PCA, feature selection, one-hot encoding statistics. All must fit on training data only.

K-Fold Cross-Validation

A single train/test split can be lucky or unlucky. K-fold CV gives a reliable estimate by rotating which part of the data is the test set — every point gets tested exactly once.

# K=5 illustration:
# Fold 1: [TEST ][train][train][train][train] → score 1
# Fold 2: [train][TEST ][train][train][train] → score 2
# Fold 3: [train][train][TEST ][train][train] → score 3
# Fold 4: [train][train][train][TEST ][train] → score 4
# Fold 5: [train][train][train][train][TEST ] → score 5
# Final = mean(scores) ± std(scores)
Standard KFold

Splits data into k equal folds randomly. Fast. Use for regression or balanced classification.

StratifiedKFold

Each fold preserves the same class proportion as the full dataset. Essential for imbalanced classification — ensures rare classes appear in every fold.

Typical choices: k=5 or k=10. Larger k = less biased estimate, more compute. Standard deviation across folds tells you how stable the model is — high std means results depend heavily on which data it saw.

cross_validate — All Metrics in One Call

Instead of writing loops manually, cross_validate handles splitting, training, and evaluating for you across all folds.

from sklearn.model_selection import cross_validate, StratifiedKFold
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
import pandas as pd

model = KNeighborsClassifier()
my_cv  = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5)

# Define all the metrics you want measured per fold
my_metrics = {
    'accuracy':  'accuracy',
    'precision': 'precision_macro',
    'recall':    'recall_macro',
    'f1':        'f1_macro'
}

scores = cross_validate(model, x, y, cv=my_cv, scoring=my_metrics)

# Put results in a DataFrame — one row per fold
df = pd.DataFrame(scores)
print(df[['test_accuracy', 'test_precision', 'test_recall', 'test_f1']])

What's happening: You never call .fit() or .predict() yourself. cross_validate does all of it — splits data, trains on k-1 folds, evaluates on the remaining fold, repeats k times. Returns a dictionary of arrays (one value per fold per metric). _macro means average the metric equally across all classes regardless of class size.

Reporting CV Results — Mean ± Std

With k folds you have k scores, not one. Always report both the mean and standard deviation.

# Simple mean/std summary
scores_df = pd.DataFrame(scores).drop(['fit_time', 'score_time'], axis=1)

print(scores_df.agg(['mean', 'std']).round(3))

# Visualise with error bars showing std
import seaborn as sns
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

plt.figure(figsize=(10, 4))
sns.barplot(
    data=scores_df,
    errorbar='sd',           # show std as error bar, not confidence interval
    palette='pastel',
    capsize=0.1,
    order=['test_f1', 'test_precision', 'test_recall', 'test_accuracy']
)
plt.title('Model performance across CV folds')
plt.ylabel('Score'); plt.xticks(rotation=45); plt.show()

Reading the results: A model with mean=0.88, std=0.02 is more trustworthy than one with mean=0.91, std=0.12. The first is consistently good. The second is erratic — its performance depends heavily on which data it happened to train on. High std is a warning sign worth investigating before trusting the mean.

GridSearchCV — Hyperparameter Tuning with CV

Never tune hyperparameters against your test set. GridSearchCV does all tuning inside the training data using cross-validation, then you evaluate the winner on the test set once at the end.

from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV, StratifiedKFold
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier

model  = KNeighborsClassifier()
my_cv  = StratifiedKFold(n_splits=5)

# 5 values × 1 hyperparameter × 5 folds = 25 models trained
hyperparams = {'n_neighbors': [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]}

# refit=False when using multiple scoring metrics
gridsearch = GridSearchCV(
    estimator=model,
    param_grid=hyperparams,
    scoring={'accuracy': 'accuracy', 'f1': 'f1_macro'},
    cv=my_cv,
    refit=False
)
gridsearch.fit(x, y)

# Results: mean score per metric per hyperparameter combination
grid_df = pd.DataFrame(gridsearch.cv_results_)
print(grid_df[['param_n_neighbors', 'mean_test_accuracy', 'mean_test_f1']])

What's happening: GridSearchCV trains a model for every combination in your grid, evaluates each with k-fold CV, and stores all results in cv_results_. refit=False is needed when you pass multiple scoring metrics — sklearn can't automatically pick a "best" model when there are multiple scores. When you use a single metric, omit refit=False and you get best_estimator_ directly.

Getting the Best Model (Single Metric)

# With a single scoring metric, GridSearchCV refits the best model automatically
gridsearch = GridSearchCV(
    KNeighborsClassifier(),
    {'n_neighbors': [2, 3, 4, 5]},
    cv=StratifiedKFold(5),
    scoring='f1_macro'     # single metric → best_estimator_ available
)
gridsearch.fit(x, y)

print(gridsearch.best_estimator_)   # best model, ready to use
print(gridsearch.best_params_)      # {'n_neighbors': 4}
print(gridsearch.best_score_)       # best mean CV F1

# Final evaluation on the held-out test set
print(classification_report(y_test, gridsearch.predict(x_test)))

GridSearchCV vs RandomizedSearchCV

GridSearchCVRandomizedSearchCV
What it triesEvery single combinationA random sample of combinations
When to useSmall grids (< ~200 combos)Large grids or continuous ranges
SpeedCan be very slow on large gridsMuch faster for same budget
QualityGuaranteed to find best in gridNear-optimal with far fewer runs
Key paramparam_grid (dict of lists)n_iter controls how many combos to try
How many models does GridSearchCV train?

Combinations × folds. Tuning 3 hyperparameters with 5 values each, 10-fold CV: 5³ × 10 = 1,250 models. With 6 hyperparameters at 10 values: 10⁶ × 10 = 10 million. That's when RandomizedSearchCV becomes necessary.

sklearn Pipeline — Preventing Leakage Inside CV

When GridSearchCV runs, it fits your model on each training fold. But if your scaler was fitted before GridSearchCV, it already saw all folds — including the test fold for each iteration. Contaminated.

The fix: wrap preprocessing and model in a Pipeline. GridSearchCV refits the entire pipeline on each training fold, guaranteeing the scaler never sees test-fold data.

from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression

pipe = Pipeline([
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),    # step 1 — name: 'scaler'
    ('model',  LogisticRegression())  # step 2 — name: 'model'
])

# Tune model parameters using __ (double underscore) to reference inside pipeline
grid = GridSearchCV(pipe, {'model__C': [0.01, 0.1, 1, 10]}, cv=5, scoring='f1')
grid.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Final evaluation
print(classification_report(y_test, grid.predict(X_test)))

What's happening: Inside each CV fold, pipe.fit(X_train_fold) triggers scaler.fit_transform(X_train_fold) then model.fit(X_train_scaled). The held-out fold only ever sees scaler.transform() — never fit_transform(). The model__C syntax means "the parameter C of the step named model".

The Complete Professional Workflow

from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split, GridSearchCV, StratifiedKFold
from sklearn.pipeline import Pipeline
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report
import pandas as pd

# Step 1 — carve off test set, never touch it again until the end
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.2, stratify=y, random_state=42
)

# Step 2 — build Pipeline (scaler + model together)
pipe = Pipeline([
    ('scaler', StandardScaler()),
    ('model',  LogisticRegression(max_iter=1000))
])

# Step 3 — tune hyperparameters inside training data only
param_grid = {'model__C': [0.01, 0.1, 1, 10, 100]}
grid = GridSearchCV(pipe, param_grid, cv=StratifiedKFold(5), scoring='f1')
grid.fit(X_train, y_train)

print("Best params:", grid.best_params_)
print("Best CV F1:",  grid.best_score_)

# Step 4 — final evaluation on the held-out test set (do this ONCE)
print(classification_report(y_test, grid.predict(X_test)))

The four rules of this workflow:
1. Split train/test first, before anything else.
2. All preprocessing is inside a Pipeline — never fitted on the full dataset.
3. All tuning happens inside GridSearchCV using only training data.
4. The test set is evaluated exactly once at the very end. Its score is your final reported result.

🧪 Quick Check

🎯 Multiclass Classification

Predicting one of three or more classes — and how to extend binary classifiers to do it.

Binary vs Multiclass

Everything learned so far (logistic regression, SVM, naive bayes) was designed for binary classification — exactly 2 classes. Multiclass means predicting one of 3 or more classes:

Binary examples

Spam / not spam · Delayed / not delayed · Cancer / no cancer

Multiclass examples

Glass type (6 classes) · Handwritten digit (0–9) · Disease diagnosis (healthy / condition A / B / C)

The challenge: most classifiers output a single score (class 0 vs class 1). Extending to K classes requires a strategy.

Two Types of Models

Native multiclass (just works)Why
Decision TreeEach leaf can be any class — splits recursively on all K classes
KNNMajority vote among K neighbours — vote goes to any class
Random ForestEnsemble of trees — same reason as Decision Tree
Naive BayesComputes P(class|features) for each class, picks highest
Binary-only (needs a strategy)Default sklearn strategy
Logistic RegressionOne-vs-Rest (OvR)
SVM (LinearSVC)One-vs-Rest (OvR)

Strategy 1: One-vs-Rest (OvR)

Train K separate binary classifiers, one per class. Each answers: "Is this example my class, or NOT my class?" At prediction time, all K classifiers give a confidence score — whichever is most confident wins.

# 6 glass types → 6 classifiers:
Classifier 1 (containers):  "I'm 15% confident"
Classifier 2 (tableware):   "I'm  8% confident"
Classifier 3 (headlamps):   "I'm 72% confident"  ← highest → prediction
Classifier 4 (vehicle):     "I'm 31% confident"
Classifier 5 (float):       "I'm 44% confident"
Classifier 6 (non-float):   "I'm 20% confident"

Final prediction → headlamps
These scores don't sum to 1 — you're doing a relative comparison (who is most confident), not computing true probabilities. That's why softmax (multinomial LR) is sometimes better for logistic regression.

Strategy 2: One-vs-One (OvO)

Train one binary classifier for every pair of classes. For K classes: K(K−1)/2 classifiers. Each test example goes to all classifiers — the class that wins the most head-to-head duels wins overall.

# 6 classes → 6×5/2 = 15 classifiers, e.g.:
containers vs tableware   → containers wins
containers vs headlamps   → headlamps wins
headlamps  vs vehicle     → headlamps wins
headlamps  vs float       → headlamps wins
... (all 15 duels)

Win counts: headlamps=4, float=4, containers=3, tableware=3 ...
Final prediction → headlamps (or float — tie broken by confidence margin)

Why OvO can be better: each classifier only ever sees 2 balanced classes — no "1 class vs all others" imbalance. Classes are learned more cleanly.

OvR vs OvO Comparison

OvROvO
Number of classifiersKK(K−1)/2
Training data per classifierFull dataset (imbalanced)Small subset (2 classes, balanced)
SpeedFasterSlower (more models)
Works well whenClasses well-separatedClasses overlap, small dataset
sklearn defaultYesVia OneVsOneClassifier wrapper

Evaluating Multiclass Models

Accuracy collapses everything to one number — it hides which classes are failing. You need per-class performance.

Confusion Matrix (K × K)

Rows = true class, columns = predicted class. Diagonal = correct predictions. Off-diagonal cells reveal which classes get confused with each other.

#          Predicted →
#       cont tab head veh flt non
cont  [ 12    0    0    1    0    0 ]  ← 1 container predicted as vehicle
head  [  0    0    8    0    0    2 ]  ← 2 headlamps confused with non-float

A perfect model has non-zero values only on the diagonal.

classification_report for Multiclass

#               precision  recall  f1-score  support
containers         0.80     0.92      0.86       13
tableware          0.67     0.83      0.74        6
headlamps          1.00     0.80      0.89       10
...
macro avg          0.82     0.85      0.83       43   ← equal weight per class
weighted avg       0.84     0.83      0.83       43   ← weighted by class size

Macro avg treats all classes equally — harsh when classes are imbalanced (rare class drags down the score). Weighted avg weights by class size — more realistic overall picture.

Glass Classification Practical — Full Code

import pandas as pd
from sklearn.preprocessing import StandardScaler
from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
from sklearn.svm import LinearSVC
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report, confusion_matrix
import seaborn as sns
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Load and scale — always scale for SVM and LR
data = pd.read_csv('data/glass.csv')
features = ['RI', 'Na', 'Mg', 'Al', 'Si', 'K', 'Ca', 'Ba', 'Fe']
data[features] = StandardScaler().fit_transform(data[features])

X, y = data[features], data['Type']

# Only 214 rows — use 80/20 split with stratify
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, test_size=0.2, random_state=5, stratify=y)

# Train and compare multiple models
models = {
    'Decision Tree': DecisionTreeClassifier(),
    'KNN':           KNeighborsClassifier(),
    'SVM':           LinearSVC(max_iter=2000, random_state=5),
    'Logistic Reg':  LogisticRegression(random_state=5)
}

for name, model in models.items():
    model.fit(X_train, y_train)
    print(f"{name}: train={model.score(X_train,y_train):.2f} test={model.score(X_test,y_test):.2f}")
# Per-class report — more informative than accuracy alone
lr = models['Logistic Reg']
print(classification_report(y_test, lr.predict(X_test)))

# Confusion matrix heatmap for all 4 models
fig, axs = plt.subplots(2, 2, figsize=(10, 10))
for (name, model), ax in zip(models.items(), axs.flatten()):
    matrix = confusion_matrix(y_test, model.predict(X_test))
    sns.heatmap(matrix, annot=True, fmt='d', cmap='Reds', ax=ax)
    ax.set_title(name)
plt.tight_layout()
# Try OvO strategy — can outperform default OvR on overlapping classes
from sklearn.multiclass import OneVsOneClassifier

ovo = OneVsOneClassifier(LinearSVC(max_iter=2000, dual="auto"))
ovo.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(classification_report(y_test, ovo.predict(X_test)))

Reading Per-Class Feature Stats

Before modelling, use .groupby().agg(['mean','std']) to understand which features separate classes:

data.groupby('Type')[features].agg(['mean', 'std'])

What to look for: a feature is useful when means are far apart between classes AND std is small within each class. For example, Barium (Ba) has mean ≈ 1.04 for headlamps but ≈ 0 for all other types — a near-perfect separator for that class.

Key Takeaways

Multiclass in practice — 5 rules
  1. Native multiclass models (trees, KNN, Naive Bayes) work out of the box — no strategy needed
  2. OvR is the sklearn default for LR and SVM — good enough for most cases
  3. Try OvO when classes overlap or dataset is small — each binary problem is cleaner and balanced
  4. Use confusion matrix — accuracy alone hides which classes are failing
  5. Stratify your split — with small datasets like glass (214 rows), stratify ensures each class is represented in train and test

🧪 Quick Check

📋 Supervised Classification — Cheat Sheet

Everything from L9–L17 in one place. Models, metrics, rules, and when to use what.

Model Comparison at a Glance

Model Needs scaling? Multiclass native? Key strength Key weakness Key params
Naive BayesNoYesFast, works well on small data and textNaive independence assumption often violatedvar_smoothing (Gaussian)
Logistic RegressionYesOvR / softmaxProbabilistic output, interpretable coefficientsLinear boundary onlyC, penalty (L1/L2), max_iter
Decision TreeNoYesInterpretable, handles mixed feature typesHigh variance — overfits easilymax_depth, min_samples_split, class_weight
Random ForestNoYesRobust, low variance, feature importanceSlow to predict, less interpretablen_estimators, max_depth, max_features
KNNYesYesNo training step, simple intuitionSlow at prediction, fails in high dimensionsn_neighbors, metric
SVM (RBF)YesOvR / OvOMax-margin, powerful for small/high-dim dataSlow on large datasets, needs tuningC, gamma, kernel
SVM (Linear)YesOvRFast, scales to large data (LinearSVC)Linear boundary onlyC, max_iter

When to Use Which Model

SituationRecommended modelWhy
Very small dataset (<500 rows)Naive Bayes or SVM (RBF)Both generalise well with limited data
Text / high-dimensional featuresNaive Bayes or LinearSVCBoth handle sparse, high-dim data natively
Need an interpretable modelLogistic Regression or Decision TreeCoefficients / tree structure are human-readable
Non-linear patterns, moderate dataRandom Forest or SVM (RBF)Both capture non-linearity without deep models
Large dataset (>100k rows)Logistic Regression or LinearSVCBoth scale linearly; SVC (kernel) is O(n²–n³)
Imbalanced classesAny + class_weight='balanced'Adjusts loss to stop model ignoring minority class
Quick baselineLogistic RegressionFast, reliable, gives probability outputs
Best possible accuracy (not interpretability)Random Forest or tuned SVMEnsemble / kernel methods beat linear models on complex data

Scaling — Must vs Not Needed

Always scaleNever need scaling
Logistic RegressionDecision Tree
SVM (all kernels)Random Forest
KNNNaive Bayes
⚠️ Always wrap scaler in a Pipeline when using cross-validation or GridSearchCV. Fitting the scaler outside the CV loop leaks test data statistics into training.

Evaluation Metrics Reference

The Confusion Matrix

Predicted PositivePredicted Negative
Actually PositiveTP — True Positive ✓FN — False Negative ✗ (missed it)
Actually NegativeFP — False Positive ✗ (false alarm)TN — True Negative ✓

The Four Key Metrics

MetricFormulaAnswersUse when
Accuracy(TP+TN) / totalHow often is the model right overall?Balanced classes only
PrecisionTP / (TP+FP)When it predicts positive, how often is it right?FP is costly (spam filter, fraud alert)
RecallTP / (TP+FN)Of all actual positives, how many did it catch?FN is costly (cancer screening, fraud detection)
F1 Score2×(P×R)/(P+R)Balanced trade-off between precision and recallImbalanced classes, when both matter

ROC-AUC

Plots True Positive Rate vs False Positive Rate across all thresholds. AUC = area under this curve. AUC = 1.0 is perfect, AUC = 0.5 is no better than random. Use when you need to evaluate the model's ranking ability independent of a fixed threshold.

Multiclass Averaging

Average typeWhat it meansUse when
Macro avgEqual weight per class — rare classes matter as much as common onesAll classes are equally important regardless of size
Weighted avgWeighted by class size — large classes dominateReflects real-world distribution of predictions
Per-classIndividual precision/recall/F1 per classDiagnosing which specific classes the model fails on

Model Deep Dives

Naive Bayes (L9b)

What it does: Uses Bayes' Rule to compute P(class|features) ∝ P(features|class) × P(class). Assumes all features are conditionally independent given the class (the "naive" assumption).

Gaussian NB: assumes each feature follows a normal distribution within each class. Estimates mean and variance per feature per class from training data.

Key params: var_smoothing — adds a small value to variances to avoid zero-probability issues (like Laplace smoothing for continuous features).

Pros: Fast to train and predict · Works well on small datasets · Handles high-dimensional text features · Native multiclass

Cons: Naive independence assumption is rarely true in practice · Can be overconfident · Poor probability calibration

Logistic Regression (L10)

What it does: Passes a linear combination of features through the sigmoid function σ(z) = 1/(1+e⁻ᶻ) to output a probability between 0 and 1. Optimises cross-entropy loss via gradient descent.

Key params: C (inverse regularisation — higher C = less regularisation = risk of overfitting) · penalty ('l2' shrinks all weights, 'l1' sets small weights to zero — feature selection) · max_iter (increase if convergence warning)

Pros: Fast · Probabilistic output · Interpretable coefficients · Good baseline · Works well on linearly separable data

Cons: Can only learn linear decision boundaries · Needs scaling · Struggles with highly non-linear patterns

Decision Trees (L12)

What it does: Recursively splits data using the feature+threshold that best reduces impurity. Each internal node is a question, each leaf is a prediction.

Splitting criteria: Gini impurity (minimise) — default in sklearn. Information gain/entropy (maximise) — alternative. Both give similar results.

Gini impurity: 1 − Σpᵢ² — 0 means a pure node (all one class), 0.5 is maximally impure (50/50 split for binary).

Key params: max_depth · min_samples_split · min_samples_leaf · class_weight='balanced'

Overfitting symptoms: 100% training accuracy + low test accuracy · Very deep tree · Poor recall on minority class

Pros: No scaling needed · Interpretable · Handles mixed types · Native multiclass

Cons: High variance — small data changes cause very different trees · Overfits without constraints

Random Forest (L13)

What it does: Trains many decision trees, each on a random bootstrap sample of data and a random subset of features at each split. Prediction = majority vote across all trees.

Why it works: Each tree is high-variance but low-bias. Averaging many uncorrelated trees cancels out individual errors — the ensemble is stable.

Key params: n_estimators (more trees = better, up to diminishing returns, never overfits) · max_depth · max_features (feature randomness) · class_weight

Pros: Low variance · Robust to overfitting · feature_importances_ built-in · No scaling needed · Native multiclass

Cons: Slow to predict · Less interpretable than a single tree · Large memory footprint

KNN (L14)

What it does: Stores all training data. For a new point, finds the K nearest training examples (by Euclidean distance) and takes a majority vote. No explicit training step — "lazy learner".

Key params: n_neighbors K (small K = overfit / jagged boundary, large K = underfit / smooth boundary) · metric (euclidean, manhattan)

Curse of dimensionality: In high dimensions, all points become approximately equidistant — "nearest" stops being meaningful. KNN degrades above ~20 features.

K=1 training accuracy: Always 100% (each point is its own nearest neighbour) — meaningless, never use training accuracy with KNN.

Pros: No training · Simple · Non-linear boundary · Native multiclass · Probability output via vote proportions

Cons: Slow prediction O(n) · Needs scaling · Fails in high dimensions · Large memory (stores all training data)

SVM (L15)

What it does: Finds the hyperplane that maximises the margin — the perpendicular gap between the boundary and the nearest training points (support vectors). Only support vectors define the model.

C parameter: Inverse of regularisation. High C = narrow margin, few violations, risk of overfitting. Low C = wide margin, more violations, simpler model.

Kernel trick: Maps data to a higher-dimensional space where it's linearly separable — without computing the transformation directly. RBF kernel: K(x,x') = exp(−γ‖x−x'‖²).

Gamma (RBF only): High γ = narrow influence per point → wiggly boundary → overfit. Low γ = wide influence → smooth boundary → underfit. Always tune C and γ together.

Pros: Globally optimal solution · Works in high dimensions · Memory efficient (only support vectors) · Kernel trick handles non-linearity

Cons: O(n²–n³) — very slow on large data · Needs scaling · No probability output without probability=True (slower) · Hard to interpret

Model Validation (L16)

Bias–Variance tradeoff: Total Error = Bias² + Variance + Irreducible Noise. High bias = underfitting (model too simple). High variance = overfitting (model memorises training data).

K-fold cross-validation: Split data into K folds. Train on K−1, validate on 1. Rotate K times. Report mean ± std of the metric. Use StratifiedKFold for classification (preserves class balance in each fold).

GridSearchCV: Exhaustively tries all hyperparameter combinations × K folds. n_jobs=-1 runs in parallel. Use refit=False when scoring with multiple metrics.

Pipeline rule: Always wrap scaler + model in a Pipeline before GridSearchCV. Prevents data leakage — scaler is refit on each training fold only.

Data leakage: Using information from the test set during training (e.g., fitting a scaler on all data before splitting). Produces optimistic metrics that don't hold on new data.

Multiclass Classification (L17)

Native multiclass: Decision Tree, KNN, Random Forest, Naive Bayes — no strategy needed.

One-vs-Rest (OvR): K binary classifiers, one per class. At prediction, highest confidence score wins. Default in sklearn for LR and SVM.

One-vs-One (OvO): K(K−1)/2 classifiers, one per pair. Each is a head-to-head duel — class with most wins is predicted. Cleaner learning per classifier (balanced 2-class problem).

Confusion matrix: K×K grid. Rows = true class, columns = predicted. Diagonal = correct. Off-diagonal reveals which classes are confused with each other.

Macro avg: equal weight per class. Weighted avg: weighted by class size. Big gap between them → rare classes are being missed.

Regularisation Reference

TypePenaltyEffectUse when
L2 (Ridge)β²Shrinks all weights toward zero — none become exactly zeroDefault choice, most situations
L1 (Lasso)|β|Sets small weights to exactly zero — automatic feature selectionYou have many irrelevant features
C in LR/SVMinverse of λHigh C = less regularisation. Low C = more regularisationTune via GridSearchCV

Class Imbalance — What to Do

ProblemFix
Model predicts majority class onlyclass_weight='balanced' in any sklearn classifier
Accuracy looks high but minority class has 0 recallSwitch metric to F1, precision, recall or ROC-AUC
Very rare positive classLower the decision threshold (e.g. 0.3 instead of 0.5) to increase recall
Evaluating on imbalanced dataAlways use StratifiedKFold so each fold preserves the class ratio

Overfitting vs Underfitting — Quick Reference

Overfitting (high variance)Underfitting (high bias)
SymptomHigh train score, low test scoreLow train score, low test score
Decision Tree fixReduce max_depth, increase min_samples_splitIncrease max_depth
SVM fixReduce C, reduce gammaIncrease C, increase gamma
KNN fixIncrease KDecrease K
LR fixReduce C (more regularisation)Increase C (less regularisation)
Random Forest fixReduce max_depth, increase min_samples_leafIncrease n_estimators or depth

The Golden Rules

Rules that apply to every classification problem
  1. Scale before KNN, SVM, and Logistic Regression — all three are distance- or gradient-based
  2. Always use a Pipeline when combining preprocessing with CV or GridSearchCV — prevents data leakage
  3. Never evaluate on training data — use a held-out test set or cross-validation
  4. Check class balance first — if imbalanced, use class_weight='balanced' and report F1/recall, not just accuracy
  5. Look at per-class metrics — a high overall accuracy can hide a model that completely misses a rare class
  6. Tune C and gamma together for SVM — they interact; tuning one without the other is unreliable
  7. Decision Trees overfit by default — always constrain with max_depth or min_samples_split
  8. Use StratifiedKFold for classification — standard KFold can produce folds with missing classes

⚡ Boosting & AdaBoost

Train weak learners sequentially — each one fixing what the previous got wrong. Bias melts away, round by round.

💡 Unlike bagging (parallel, reduces variance), boosting is sequential and targets bias reduction.

Core Idea

Boosting trains models one at a time. After each model, the training data is re-weighted — samples the current ensemble got wrong receive higher weight, so the next model is forced to focus on them.

Sequential training

Each stump is trained after seeing the mistakes of all previous stumps — not in parallel like bagging.

Weighted vote

Stumps with lower error get higher weight α in the final vote. Better stumps have more say.

AdaBoost — Step by Step

The diagram below walks through two full rounds using a 5-sample dataset (Age, BMI, Gender → Risk label). Follow the flow left to right, top to bottom.

Round 1 dataset → Stump 1 → weight update → Round 2 dataset → Stump 2 → final weighted vote

The Algorithm

  1. Initialise equal sample weights: wᵢ = 1/N
  2. Train a weak learner (decision stump) on the weighted data
  3. Calculate weighted error: ε = Σ wᵢ · 𝟙[prediction ≠ label]
  4. Calculate stump weight: α = ½ · ln((1−ε) / ε)
  5. Update sample weights — misclassified samples go up, correct ones go down
  6. Normalise weights so they sum to 1, repeat from step 2
Final prediction

F(x) = sign( α₁·h₁(x) + α₂·h₂(x) + … + αₖ·hₖ(x) ) — each stump's vote is weighted by how accurate it was.

Weight Update Formula

Sample resultNew weightEffect
✓ Correctwᵢ · e−αWeight decreases — already well-handled
✗ Wrongwᵢ · e+αWeight increases — next stump must focus here

After updating, all weights are normalised so they sum to 1 (divide by Z = Σ raw weights).

Stump Weight α

α controls how much each stump contributes to the final vote:

ε → 0 (near perfect)

α → ∞. Stump gets huge weight — it rarely makes mistakes.

ε = 0.5 (random)

α = 0. Stump gets zero weight — equivalent to random guessing, ignored.

AdaBoost vs Gradient Boosting

AdaBoostGradient Boosting
Re-weightingSample weights (exponential)Fit to pseudo-residuals (loss gradient)
Loss functionFixed: exponential lossAny differentiable loss (MSE, log loss…)
Base learner depthStumps (depth=1)Shallow trees (depth 3–8 typical)
Librariessklearn AdaBoostClassifierXGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost

Boosting in Practice

Pros

  • Very high predictive performance
  • Works well on structured/tabular data
  • Feature importance available
  • Can use any weak learner

Cons

  • Prone to overfitting on noisy data
  • Hard to parallelise (sequential by nature)
  • Sensitive to outliers (outliers get upweighted)
  • More hyperparameters to tune
from sklearn.ensemble import AdaBoostClassifier
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier

# AdaBoost with decision stumps (depth=1)
ada = AdaBoostClassifier(
    estimator=DecisionTreeClassifier(max_depth=1),
    n_estimators=100,
    learning_rate=1.0,
    random_state=42
)
ada.fit(X_train, y_train)
ada.score(X_test, y_test)

# Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) — more powerful in practice
from xgboost import XGBClassifier
xgb = XGBClassifier(n_estimators=200, max_depth=4, learning_rate=0.1)
xgb.fit(X_train, y_train)
⚠️ AdaBoost is sensitive to noisy data and outliers — they get upweighted repeatedly, potentially dominating the model. Use robust preprocessing or switch to Gradient Boosting for noisy datasets.

🧪 Practical: Predicting Online Purchase Intent with LightGBM

We classify e-commerce visitors as likely to purchase or not, using 12,330 sessions from the UCI Online Shoppers dataset. Binary target: Revenue (True = made a purchase).

LightGBM is a gradient boosting library optimised for speed and memory. It handles categorical features natively — no one-hot encoding needed.

Dataset Overview

Feature groupExamplesType
Page metricsAdministrative, Informational, ProductRelated (count + duration)Numeric
AnalyticsBounceRates, ExitRates, PageValuesNumeric (float)
Session contextMonth, VisitorType, Weekend, SpecialDayCategorical / bool
TechnicalOperatingSystems, Browser, Region, TrafficTypeCategorical (int codes)
TargetRevenueBoolean (True / False)

Step 1 — Load Data & Mark Categoricals

LightGBM requires data to be int, float, or bool. Columns that are truly categorical (even if stored as integers like Region or Browser) must be cast to category dtype so LightGBM treats them correctly instead of assuming numeric ordering.

import pandas as pd

data = pd.read_csv('data/online_shoppers_intention.csv')

# Cast all categorical columns — both string types AND integer-coded categories
# e.g. Region=1 doesn't mean "less than" Region=3 — it's just a label
cat_cols = ['Month', 'VisitorType', 'OperatingSystems',
            'Browser', 'Region', 'TrafficType']
data[cat_cols] = data[cat_cols].astype('category')

data.info()    # confirm dtypes — category columns now show as CategoricalDtype

Step 2 — Train/Test Split with Stratification

Only ~15% of sessions result in a purchase. Without stratify=y, a random split could put nearly all buyers in one set.

from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

X = data.drop('Revenue', axis=1)
y = data.Revenue                        # boolean: True=purchase, False=no purchase

# stratify=y ensures both splits have the same ~15% purchase rate
X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, stratify=y, random_state=136
)

Step 3 — Create LightGBM Datasets

LightGBM uses its own Dataset format instead of raw arrays. The reference=lgb_train argument on the test set is critical — it tells LightGBM to use the same categorical encoding that was built from training data, preventing data leakage.

import lightgbm as lgb

lgb_train = lgb.Dataset(X_train, y_train)

# reference=lgb_train: share the categorical encoding from train — same bins and category maps
# Without this, test set gets its own encoding, which can differ and cause silent errors
lgb_test  = lgb.Dataset(X_test, y_test, reference=lgb_train)

Step 4 — Configure Parameters

ParameterValueWhat it means
boosting_type'gbdt'Gradient Boosting Decision Trees — the standard algorithm
objective'binary'Binary classification (log loss objective)
metriclistWhat to track/report: logloss, AUC, average precision
num_leaves31Max leaves per tree — controls complexity. More leaves = more expressive but more overfit risk
learning_rate0.1How much each new tree contributes. Smaller = more trees needed but better generalisation
max_depth-1No depth limit — tree depth is controlled via num_leaves instead
seed0Reproducibility — same results every run
params = {
    'boosting_type': 'gbdt',          # gradient boosted decision trees
    'objective':     'binary',         # binary classification task
    'metric':        ['binary_logloss', 'auc', 'average_precision'],
    'num_leaves':    31,               # max leaves per tree — main complexity control
    'learning_rate': 0.1,             # shrinkage: lower = slower but more stable
    'max_depth':     -1,              # -1 = unlimited; num_leaves controls depth implicitly
    'verbose':       0,               # 0 = warnings only, -1 = silent
    'seed':          0,               # reproducibility
}

Step 5 — Train & Monitor Seen vs Unseen Loss

By passing both lgb_train and lgb_test to valid_sets, we track the loss curve on training and test simultaneously. The gap between them reveals overfitting.

# evals dict stores metric values at each boosting round — used for plotting
evals = {}

model = lgb.train(
    params=params,
    train_set=lgb_train,
    num_boost_round=200,              # number of boosting iterations (trees to build)
    valid_sets=[lgb_train, lgb_test],  # monitor both sets every round
    valid_names=['seen', 'unseen'],   # labels for the plot
    callbacks=[
        lgb.record_evaluation(evals),  # saves metrics into evals dict each round
        lgb.log_evaluation(10)         # prints metrics every 10 rounds
    ]
)

# Plot all 3 metrics across 200 rounds for seen vs unseen
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
f, a = plt.subplots(1, 3, figsize=(16, 5))
for e, m in enumerate(['binary_logloss', 'auc', 'average_precision']):
    lgb.plot_metric(evals, metric=m, ax=a[e], title=f"{m} during training")
plt.tight_layout()
Key observation — overfitting

The model overfits quickly — 200 rounds is too many. The unseen loss starts rising while training loss keeps dropping. Peak unseen performance is around 50–70 iterations. Fix: use fewer rounds or add early stopping.

Step 6 — Feature Importance: split vs gain

LightGBM provides two importance types — always check both, they tell different stories:

split

How many times a feature was used to make a split across all trees. High split count = the model relies on this feature heavily to make decisions.

gain

Total improvement in the loss function brought by all splits on this feature. High gain = this feature produces the biggest quality improvements when split on. More meaningful than split count alone.

for imp_type in ['split', 'gain']:
    lgb.plot_importance(model,
                         importance_type=imp_type,
                         dpi=100,
                         title=f"Feature importance by {imp_type}")

# Result: PageValues, ExitRates, ProductRelated_Duration dominate both metrics
# Human activity features (time spent, pages visited) are far more predictive than technical ones (OS, Browser)

Step 7 — Predicting on New Data

LightGBM's model.predict() returns probabilities (0–1), not class labels. We need a helper function to apply a threshold and convert to 0/1.

import numpy as np

def predict_class(model, data, threshold=0.5):
    y_prob = model.predict(data)            # returns float probabilities e.g. [0.12, 0.87, 0.43]
    return np.where(y_prob > threshold, 1, 0) # above threshold → 1 (purchase), else 0
    # Lower threshold → catch more buyers (higher recall, lower precision)
    # Higher threshold → fewer false positives (higher precision, lower recall)

from sklearn.metrics import accuracy_score
print("Test accuracy:", accuracy_score(y_test, predict_class(model, X_test)))
# Train accuracy ~0.99 vs test ~0.88 — large gap confirms overfitting with 200 rounds

Step 8 — Hyperparameter Tuning with GridSearchCV

lgb.LGBMClassifier is the sklearn-compatible API — works with GridSearchCV exactly like any sklearn model.

ParameterValues triedWhat it controls
max_bin155, 255Number of bins for continuous features. More bins = more precise splits but slower. Default 255.
num_leaves7, 15, 31Max leaves per tree. Lower = simpler, less overfit. Higher = more expressive, more overfit risk.
min_data_in_leaf15, 20Minimum samples in a leaf. Higher = smoother, more generalised model.
min_gain_to_split0, 0.01Minimum gain required to make a split. Positive value prunes uninformative splits.
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV

params_grid = dict(
    max_bin=[155, 255],          # bins for continuous features — more = slower but more precise
    num_leaves=[7, 15, 31],       # tree complexity — 7 is simple/robust, 31 is default
    min_data_in_leaf=[15, 20],    # minimum samples per leaf — higher prevents tiny overfit leaves
    min_gain_to_split=[0, 0.01], # prune splits below this gain threshold — 0 = no pruning
    verbose=[-1],                 # suppress per-fold output
    seed=[0],
)

# LGBMClassifier = sklearn-compatible LightGBM — works with GridSearchCV, Pipeline, cross_validate
classifier = lgb.LGBMClassifier()

# 2×3×2×2 = 24 combinations × 5 CV folds = 120 models trained in parallel (n_jobs=-1)
gcv = GridSearchCV(estimator=classifier, param_grid=params_grid, n_jobs=-1, verbose=1)
gcv.fit(X_train, y_train)

Step 9 — Evaluate Best Model

print(f"Best CV accuracy:     {gcv.best_score_:.4f}")
print(f"Best hyperparameters: {gcv.best_params_}")

best_model = gcv.best_estimator_

print(f"Train accuracy: {best_model.score(X_train, y_train):.4f}")
print(f"Test  accuracy: {best_model.score(X_test,  y_test ):.4f}")
# Train ≈ test after tuning — overfitting resolved. Accuracy up to ~90.7%

# Per-class breakdown — critical because only ~15% of sessions are purchases
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report, confusion_matrix

best_pred = best_model.predict(X_test)       # LGBMClassifier.predict() returns class labels directly
print(classification_report(y_test, best_pred))
print(confusion_matrix(y_test, best_pred))

# Feature importances from the sklearn-compatible best model
f_imp = pd.DataFrame(dict(
    feature=best_model.feature_name_,         # column names used during training
    importance=best_model.feature_importances_ # importance_type set by best_model.importance_type
)).sort_values('importance', ascending=False)
print(f_imp.head(10))

Key Learnings from the Practical

No encoding needed

LightGBM handles categorical columns natively via .astype('category'). No get_dummies() required — it finds optimal splits across categories directly.

Overfitting is fast

200 boosting rounds badly overfit this data. Training accuracy hit ~99% while test stayed ~88%. Peak unseen performance was around 50–70 rounds. Always monitor both curves.

PageValues is #1

How much a page was "worth" (based on conversion data) is by far the strongest signal — makes sense, users who land on high-value pages are further down the purchase funnel.

Tuning fixed overfitting

GridSearchCV brought train and test accuracy close together (~90.7% both). min_data_in_leaf and smaller num_leaves were key — they constrained tree complexity.

LightGBM vs sklearn GBT — when to use which

LightGBMsklearn GradientBoostingClassifier
SpeedVery fast (histogram-based)Slower on large datasets
CategoricalsNative supportNeeds encoding
APINative API + LGBMClassifier (sklearn)sklearn only
Use whenLarge data, speed matters, tabular dataSmall-medium data, familiarity

🧪 Quick Check

🧩 Introduction to Ensemble Methods

Combine many weak models into one strong one — the core idea behind Random Forests, Gradient Boosting, and Stacking.

💡 "Ensemble" is the meta-technique. Bagging, Boosting, and Stacking are three different ways to build one.

The Core Idea

Instead of training one strong model (which is hard to achieve), ensemble methods combine many weak learners — models that are only slightly better than random guessing — into a powerful collective prediction.

Wisdom of Crowds

No single person can exactly guess the weight of a cow, but averaging many independent guesses is surprisingly accurate. Ensembles work the same way: diverse models cover each other's mistakes.

A Model's Output Changes Based on Two Things

Hyperparameters

Set by you before training — e.g. max depth, regularisation strength. Fixed across runs.

Learned Parameters

Estimated from the training data. Different data → different parameters → different predictions. Ensembles exploit this.

3 Components of Every Ensemble

  1. Base model — the weak learner (usually a decision tree)
  2. Meta-algorithm — how to generate diverse instances of the base model
  3. Combination strategy — how to merge individual predictions into a final decision

3 Types of Ensemble Methods

TypeTrainingDataCombinationExample
BaggingParallelRandom subsets (bootstrap)Equal vote / meanRandom Forest
BoostingSequentialAll data, re-weightedWeighted voteXGBoost, AdaBoost
StackingAnyDifferent model familiesMeta-learner modelNetflix Prize winner

Bagging — How It Works

The diagram below shows the full Bagging / Random Forest flow. Each bootstrap sample trains a Decision Tree on a different subset of rows and features. A majority vote across all trees gives the final prediction.

Dataset → bootstrap samples → diverse trees → aggregate → final prediction

Why Does It Work? Bias–Variance Tradeoff

High Bias (underfitting)

Model is too simple — misses real patterns. Low training AND test accuracy.

High Variance (overfitting)

Model memorises noise — great on training, poor on test. Bagging directly reduces variance by averaging.

When many models each overfit differently, their errors are uncorrelated. Averaging them lets errors cancel out while the true signal accumulates — variance drops dramatically.

Suitable Base Models

Ensembles work best with unstable base models — ones that change significantly when training data changes. Decision trees are ideal: they overfit easily and produce very different structures on different samples.

Key intuition

A model with only 51% accuracy on binary classification becomes very powerful in an ensemble. If 1,000 independent 51%-accurate models vote, the majority vote accuracy is far above 51%.

Pros and Cons

Advantages

  • Improved predictive performance
  • Variance reduction (bagging) or bias reduction (boosting)
  • Uncertainty estimation via prediction spread
  • Feature importance still available

Disadvantages

  • Reduced interpretability ("black box")
  • Additional training overhead
  • Boosting is hard to parallelise
  • More hyperparameters to tune
from sklearn.ensemble import BaggingClassifier, RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier

# Manual bagging with any base model
bag = BaggingClassifier(estimator=DecisionTreeClassifier(), n_estimators=20, random_state=42)
bag.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Random Forest = bagging + random feature subsets (highly optimised)
rf = RandomForestClassifier(n_estimators=100, max_features='sqrt', random_state=42)
rf.fit(X_train, y_train)
print(rf.feature_importances_)   # which features drove the splits

🧪 Practical: Account Churn Prediction

Can we predict which users will close their account? We have 20 behavioural features (login frequency, purchase count, etc.) per user. We compare a single Naive Bayes model against a bagging ensemble of the same base model.

Dataset: account_data.csv — binary target closed_account (1 = churned, 0 = retained)

Step 1 — Load & Explore the Data

import pandas as pd

data = pd.read_csv('data/account_data.csv')
print(data.head())
print(data['closed_account'].value_counts())
What to look for

Check the class balance — if 0s massively outnumber 1s, accuracy will be misleading. Use precision/recall to evaluate churned-account detection.

Step 2 — Feature / Target Split & Train-Test Split

from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

X = data.drop('closed_account', axis=1)   # 20 behavioural features
y = data['closed_account']                   # binary target (0 / 1)

X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    X, y, random_state=5                       # 75% train / 25% test (default)
)

Step 3 — Baseline: Single Naive Bayes Model

Establish a baseline before building the ensemble. Train on training data, evaluate on both sets to check for overfitting.

from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report

model = GaussianNB()
model.fit(X_train, y_train)

# Training performance
y_pred_train = model.predict(X_train)
print("Train accuracy:", model.score(X_train, y_train))
print(classification_report(y_train, y_pred_train))

# Test performance (unseen data)
y_pred_test = model.predict(X_test)
print("Test accuracy:", model.score(X_test, y_test))
print(classification_report(y_test, y_pred_test))

Step 4 — Bagging Ensemble of Naive Bayes Models

BaggingClassifier wraps any sklearn model. It trains n_estimators copies on bootstrap samples and combines predictions by majority vote — same interface as any sklearn model.

from sklearn.ensemble import BaggingClassifier

ensemble = BaggingClassifier(
    estimator=GaussianNB(),     # same base model as the baseline
    n_estimators=10,             # 10 models trained on different bootstrap subsets
    random_state=5
)
ensemble.fit(X_train, y_train)

y_pred_ens = ensemble.predict(X_test)
print("Ensemble test accuracy:", ensemble.score(X_test, y_test))
print(classification_report(y_test, y_pred_ens))

Step 5 — Compare Results

ModelWhat changedExpected effect
Single GaussianNBOne model, all training dataBaseline — may miss the minority (churned) class
BaggingClassifier (n=10)10 models, bootstrap subsets, majority voteHigher accuracy, better recall on churned class
Conclusion from the practical

Bagging improved performance even with the same simple Naive Bayes base model. Diverse models trained on different subsets cover different error patterns — their mistakes cancel when combined. To go further: increase n_estimators to 50–100, or swap GaussianNB for DecisionTreeClassifier to see a larger effect.

🧪 Practical 2: Kickstarter Success Prediction

Predict whether a crowdfunding project will succeed, fail, or be cancelled — a 3-class problem. We'll go from raw data → baseline → Random Forest → hyperparameter tuning → feature importance.

Dataset: kickstarter_sample.csv — 5,000 Kickstarter projects, target outcome (successful / failed / canceled)

Dataset Features

FeatureDescription
main_categoryMajor category the project belongs to
categoryMore detailed category label
goalHow much money the project wants to raise
backersNumber of people who donated when time was up
countryCountry the project came from
outcomesuccessful / failed / canceled (target)

Step 1 — Load Data & EDA

import pandas as pd

data = pd.read_csv('data/kickstarter_sample.csv')

X = data.drop(['outcome'], axis=1)
y = data['outcome']

# Class balance
print(data['outcome'].value_counts(normalize=True))

# Mean goal by outcome — do successful projects aim lower?
print(data.groupby('outcome')['goal'].mean())
Key insight from EDA

Just over half of projects fail (~53%), ~35% succeed, ~10% are cancelled. Successful projects have a much lower mean goal — ambitious targets tend to fall short.

Step 2 — Preprocessing: One-Hot Encode & Split

Categorical features (country, category, main_category) must be one-hot encoded before any sklearn model can use them.

from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split

# One-hot encode all categorical columns in one step
X = pd.get_dummies(X)

X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(X, y, random_state=5)

Step 3 — Baseline: Naive Bayes & Decision Tree

Always set a baseline before building an ensemble. Here we compare two single models so we know what the Random Forest needs to beat.

from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB
from sklearn.tree import DecisionTreeClassifier
from sklearn.metrics import classification_report

nb_model  = GaussianNB()
dtc_model = DecisionTreeClassifier(random_state=5)

nb_model.fit(X_train, y_train)
dtc_model.fit(X_train, y_train)

print(f'Naive Bayes accuracy:    {nb_model.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}')
print(f'Decision Tree accuracy:  {dtc_model.score(X_test, y_test):.3f}')
print(classification_report(y_test, nb_model.predict(X_test)))
print(classification_report(y_test, dtc_model.predict(X_test)))
What to look for

Both baselines struggle on the canceled class (rare, ~10%). Accuracy looks acceptable overall but per-class precision/recall tells the real story.

Step 4 — Random Forest (Default Hyperparameters)

from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier

rf = RandomForestClassifier(random_state=5)   # 100 trees, unlimited depth
rf.fit(X_train, y_train)

rf_y_pred = rf.predict(X_test)
print(classification_report(y_test, rf_y_pred))
Result

Accuracy is higher overall vs the baselines, but still mostly driven by the majority class (failed). The canceled class remains hard to predict — it is both rare and noisier.

Step 5 — Hyperparameter Tuning with GridSearchCV

GridSearchCV trains one model per combination of parameters × CV folds and picks the winner. Below we explain every parameter being searched.

Understanding class_weight

Our dataset is imbalanced: ~53% failed, ~35% successful, ~10% canceled. Without adjustment, the forest ignores the rare class to maximise overall accuracy. class_weight changes the misclassification penalty per class — a higher weight means the model is punished more for getting that class wrong.

Manual weights (grid approach)

Each dictionary in the list is one candidate. GridSearch tries all three and picks whichever scores best on CV. The factor of 2 is a rough nudge — not derived from data, just "let's try doubling the cost for each class and see which helps."

Computed weights (principled approach)

compute_class_weight('balanced') calculates the weight automatically from class frequencies: weight = total / (n_classes × class_count). Rare classes automatically get higher weight. No guessing required.

from sklearn.utils.class_weight import compute_class_weight
import numpy as np

# Computes weight = total_samples / (n_classes × class_count) for each class
# Rare classes automatically get higher weight — no manual tuning needed
weights = compute_class_weight(
    class_weight='balanced',          # use class frequencies to calculate weights
    classes=np.unique(y_train),       # array of unique class labels
    y=y_train                          # training labels to count frequencies from
)
# Example output: [3.1, 0.63, 0.95] → canceled gets ~3x weight because it's only 10% of data
print(dict(zip(np.unique(y_train), weights)))

All GridSearchCV Parameters Explained

ParameterWhat it controlsValues triedWhy
n_estimatorsNumber of trees in the forest100, 200, 300More trees = more stable, diminishing returns after ~200. Each value costs proportionally more time.
min_samples_leafMinimum samples required in a leaf node1, 2, 3Higher = simpler trees = less overfitting. 1 = trees can grow until each leaf has 1 sample (default, complex). 3 = each leaf must summarise at least 3 samples.
max_samplesFraction of training rows in each bootstrap sample0.25, 0.750.75 = each tree sees 75% of data (more stable). 0.25 = each tree sees 25% (more diversity, higher variance per tree but better ensemble decorrelation).
class_weightMisclassification penalty per class3 dicts (each class upweighted once)Corrects for class imbalance by making the model pay more for missing the rare class. Each dict is one candidate — GridSearch picks the winner.
from sklearn.model_selection import GridSearchCV

params = dict(

    # How many trees? More = more stable but slower to train
    n_estimators=[100, 200, 300],

    # Minimum samples per leaf — higher = simpler, less overfit trees
    min_samples_leaf=[1, 2, 3],

    # Bootstrap fraction — what % of rows each tree sees
    # 0.75 = diverse but stable  |  0.25 = very diverse, each tree only sees a quarter
    max_samples=[0.25, 0.75],

    # class_weight: misclassification penalty per class
    # Each dict is one candidate — GridSearch trains all 3 and picks the best
    # Weight of 2 = "punish getting this class wrong twice as hard"
    # 'canceled' = 10% of data, 'failed' = 53%, 'successful' = 35%
    class_weight=[
        {'canceled': 2, 'failed': 1, 'successful': 1},  # prioritise finding canceled (rarest)
        {'canceled': 1, 'failed': 2, 'successful': 1},  # prioritise finding failures
        {'canceled': 1, 'failed': 1, 'successful': 2},  # prioritise finding successes
        # Better alternative: 'balanced' — auto-computes weights from class frequencies
    ],
)

# n_jobs=-1 uses all CPU cores in parallel — training 3×3×2×3 = 54 combinations × 5 CV folds = 270 models
rf  = RandomForestClassifier(random_state=5, n_jobs=-1)
gcv = GridSearchCV(estimator=rf, param_grid=params, n_jobs=-1, verbose=2)
gcv.fit(X_train, y_train)

Step 6 — Best Model & Feature Importance

# gcv.best_score_  → mean CV accuracy of the winning combination
# gcv.best_params_ → the exact hyperparameter values that won
# gcv.best_estimator_ → the already-fitted model with those params
print(f"Best CV accuracy: {gcv.best_score_:.3f}")
print(f"Best params:      {gcv.best_params_}")

best_model = gcv.best_estimator_    # already fitted — no need to call .fit() again

# Evaluate on the held-out test set (not used in grid search)
y_pred = best_model.predict(X_test)
print(classification_report(y_test, y_pred))

# ── Feature Importance ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────
# feature_importances_ = proportion of total Gini reduction each feature contributed
# across all splits in all trees. Values sum to 1. Higher = more important.
# Align scores with column names by zipping X_train.columns
f_imp = pd.DataFrame(dict(
    feature=X_train.columns,            # column names (after get_dummies)
    score=best_model.feature_importances_  # importance score per feature
))

# Sort descending, show top 10 most influential features
print(f_imp.sort_values('score', ascending=False)[:10])
# Result: backers >> goal >> country_... >> category_...
# backers is #1 but only known at end of campaign — useful for analysis, not prediction upfront
# goal is #2 and IS known before the campaign — a genuinely predictive feature

Key Learnings from the Practical

#1 Feature: backers

Unsurprisingly the strongest predictor. More backers = more likely successful. But this is only known at the end of the campaign — limits real-world use.

#2 Feature: goal

Successful projects had a much lower mean goal. Ambitious targets tend to fail. Goal is known upfront — a genuinely useful predictor.

#3 Feature: country

Country of origin is a meaningful signal. US projects dominate the dataset; different markets have different success rates.

Tuning vs features

GridSearch gave only modest improvements. The bottleneck was feature quality, not hyperparameters — better features would help more than more trees.

Conclusion

Random Forest outperformed both single baselines out of the box. It handles categorical features (after encoding), multiclass targets, and class imbalance (via class_weight) with minimal setup. Feature importance gives interpretability even in a "black box" ensemble — you always know which features mattered most.

🧪 Quick Check

🔀 Stacking — Ensembles with a Meta-Learner

Combine entirely different model families through a second-level meta-learner that learns the optimal blend — used by Kaggle champions and the Netflix Prize winner.

💡 Stacking is the most powerful and flexible ensemble technique. Instead of averaging predictions equally, a meta-learner learns how to combine them.

Why Stacking?

Bagging and boosting combine many copies of the same model family. Stacking goes further — it combines fundamentally different model families (e.g. KNN + Random Forest + Naive Bayes) through a second-level model that learns to assign each base model its optimal weight and role.

Diverse model families

Instead of 100 decision trees (Random Forest), use KNN + Random Forest + Naive Bayes. Completely different representations capture different aspects of the data.

Meta-learner

A second-level model trained on the base models' predictions. It learns which model to trust more and in which situations — far smarter than a simple average.

When to use

When you want maximum performance and have multiple strong models. Common in Kaggle competitions and production systems where small gains matter (finance, medicine, ad-tech).

Architecture at a Glance

The 4-Step Stacking Algorithm

Stacking has a clean 4-step structure. Each step is separated to prevent data leakage between levels.

Step 1 — Train individual (base) models

Train multiple models on the training data. The models can be from entirely different classes — Random Forest, SVM, Neural Network, KNN, Naive Bayes. Diversity is the goal: models that make different errors complement each other.

You can also vary hyperparameters within the same family — e.g. two Random Forests with different depths — to create additional diversity.

Step 2 — Generate predictions on a held-out set

Use the trained base models to predict on a validation set — data the models did NOT see during training. This is critical: if you predict on training data, the meta-learner sees "too good" predictions and learns the wrong thing (data leakage).

With N base models, you get N columns of predictions. These become the new feature matrix for the meta-learner.

Step 3 — Train the meta-learner

Train a second-level model using the base models' validation predictions as inputs and the true labels as targets. For classification, Logistic Regression is a common meta-learner — it learns a weighted blend. For regression, Linear Regression works well.

More complex meta-learners (Decision Trees, Neural Networks) can capture non-linear interactions between base model predictions, but risk overfitting.

Step 4 — Validate the ensemble

To evaluate the full pipeline, use a third, separate test set that was not used in either training step. Run each base model on it, collect their predictions, run the meta-learner on those predictions, and evaluate the final output.

Cross-Validation Schemes

Getting the CV scheme right is the trickiest part of stacking. The simple approach splits data into 3 sets; K-fold variants make better use of data.

SchemeHow it worksTrade-off
Simple 2-levelSplit once: train set (level 1), val set (level 2), test set (evaluation). Easiest to implement.Less data for base model training. May underfit if data is small.
K-fold stackingTrain K ensembles using K-fold CV. Collect out-of-fold predictions for every sample → use as meta-learner input. Train final base models on full data.Uses all data. Must train base models K+1 times — expensive.
2-level K-foldApply K-fold at both levels. Uses the whole dataset for both training stages.Very data-efficient. Each model trained K₁×K₂ times — can be prohibitively slow.
2-level ensemblesStack multiple meta-learners (e.g. Linear + Neural Net) and stack again. In theory, unlimited levels.Maximum performance. Rapidly increasing complexity and compute cost.

The golden rule

Base models must never predict on data they were trained on when generating the meta-learner's training features. That would let the meta-learner see artificially good predictions (training error, not generalisation error), causing it to learn the wrong blend.

Practical — Stacking on the Iris Dataset

We compare three individual models against a stacking ensemble using mlxtend — a popular library for ensemble methods. All models are evaluated with 3-fold cross-validation on the Iris dataset (2 features: petal width, petal length).

Step 1 — Imports and Data

from sklearn.ensemble import RandomForestClassifier
from sklearn.linear_model import LogisticRegression
from sklearn.naive_bayes import GaussianNB
from sklearn.neighbors import KNeighborsClassifier
from sklearn import model_selection, datasets
from mlxtend.classifier import StackingClassifier   # Note: mlxtend, not sklearn

iris = datasets.load_iris()
X_train = iris.data[:, 1:3]   # Use only 2 features: petal width, petal length
y_train = iris.target          # 3-class labels: 0, 1, 2
mlxtend vs sklearn: sklearn also has StackingClassifier but mlxtend's version has a simpler API and was widely adopted before sklearn's version existed. The interfaces differ — mlxtend uses classifiers=[] and meta_classifier=.

Step 2 — Define Base Models (Level 1)

# Three completely different model families — the source of diversity
myclf1 = KNeighborsClassifier(n_neighbors=1)   # Instance-based, no training phase
myclf2 = RandomForestClassifier(random_state=1) # Tree ensemble — handles non-linearity
myclf3 = GaussianNB()                           # Probabilistic, fast, works with small data

Each model has fundamentally different decision boundaries. KNN uses local similarity, Random Forest uses tree splits, Naive Bayes uses probability distributions. When they disagree, the meta-learner must decide who to trust.

Step 3 — Define Meta-Learner (Level 2)

# The meta-learner receives [KNN_pred, RF_pred, NB_pred] as its input features
mylr = LogisticRegression()   # Simple, interpretable — learns optimal blend weights

Step 4 — Build the Stacking Ensemble

stackingclf = StackingClassifier(
    classifiers=[myclf1, myclf2, myclf3],  # Level-1 base models (any number)
    meta_classifier=mylr                    # Level-2 meta-learner
)

Step 5 — Compare All Models with Cross-Validation

for clf, label in zip(
    [myclf1, myclf2, myclf3, stackingclf],
    ['KNN', 'Random Forest', 'Naive Bayes', 'StackingClassifier']
):
    scores = model_selection.cross_val_score(
        clf, X_train, y_train,
        cv=3,              # 3 folds: train on 2/3, test on 1/3, repeat 3×
        scoring='accuracy'
    )
    print(f"Accuracy: {scores.mean():.3f} (+/- {scores.std():.3f}) [{label}]")

# Output:
# Accuracy: 0.913 (+/- 0.008) [KNN]
# Accuracy: 0.934 (+/- 0.051) [Random Forest]
# Accuracy: 0.921 (+/- 0.027) [Naive Bayes]
# Accuracy: 0.947 (+/- 0.033) [StackingClassifier]  ← BEST

Result

The stacking ensemble (94.7%) outperforms all individual models — KNN (91.3%), Random Forest (93.4%), and Naive Bayes (92.1%). The meta-learner learned which model to trust for which region of the feature space.

Real-World Example — Netflix Prize

The $1,000,000 Ensemble

The Netflix Prize challenged teams to improve their movie recommendation system by 10%. The winning team, BellKor's Pragmatic Chaos, was a partnership of three separate teams that won by building a massive stacking ensemble.

  • 100+ individual models: K-Nearest Neighbours, Restricted Boltzmann Machines (RBM), SVD matrix factorization, Neural Networks — all completely different approaches.
  • Residual training: Individual models were stopped when they stopped improving the ensemble predictions, not their own predictions alone.
  • Blending (their name for stacking): Started with linear stacking, then tried neural networks and polynomial functions as meta-learners. Later stacked the stacked ensembles into a 2-level meta-learner.
  • Complementary weaknesses: RBMs were not great predictors alone, but they got some samples right that all other models got wrong — making them irreplaceable in the ensemble.

Key insight: even models that are worse than the best individual model can still improve the ensemble, as long as their errors are different from the other models' errors.

Stacking vs Bagging vs Boosting — Summary

BaggingBoostingStacking
TrainingParallelSequentialParallel (base), then meta
Base modelsSame typeSame type (weak)Different families
CombinationEqual vote / avgWeighted vote (α)Meta-learner
TargetsVariance reductionBias reductionBoth (via diversity)
Data requirementMediumMediumMore (need held-out set)
ComplexityLowMediumHigh
Best forHigh-variance modelsIterative refinementMaximum performance

🧪 Quick Check

📉 Gradient Boosting

The generalisation of AdaBoost — fit each new tree to the residuals of the current ensemble, using gradient descent on any loss function.

💡 Gradient Boosting is the engine behind XGBoost, LightGBM, and CatBoost — the algorithms that win most tabular ML competitions.

From AdaBoost to Gradient Boosting

AdaBoost works by re-weighting misclassified samples and fitting stumps to those weighted samples. It implicitly minimises an exponential loss. Gradient Boosting generalises this: instead of re-weighting, each new tree is fitted directly to the negative gradient of any differentiable loss function — the pseudo-residuals.

AdaBoost

Re-weights samples. Works only with exponential loss. Stumps (depth-1 trees) only. Sensitive to outliers — misclassified points get exponentially high weight.

Gradient Boosting

Fits new trees to pseudo-residuals (negative gradient). Works with any loss function — MSE for regression, log-loss for classification. Deeper trees allowed. Far more flexible.

The Core Idea — Residual Fitting

Think of it as learning in steps. The first tree makes a rough prediction. The next tree looks at where it was wrong (the residuals) and tries to correct those errors. Repeat. Each round adds a small correction in the direction that most reduces the loss.

The Algorithm — Step by Step

Step 1 — Initialise with a constant prediction

Start with the simplest possible prediction: for regression, use the mean of y. For classification, use the log-odds of the positive class. This is F₀(x).

F₀(x) = argminγ Σ L(yᵢ, γ) — the single value that minimises total loss.

Step 2 — Compute pseudo-residuals

Calculate the negative gradient of the loss with respect to the current predictions. For MSE loss, these are just the ordinary residuals rᵢ = yᵢ − F(xᵢ). For other losses (log-loss, Huber), the formula differs but the idea is the same: "how should each prediction move to reduce the loss?"

r_ik = -∂L(yᵢ, F(xᵢ)) / ∂F(xᵢ)

# For MSE: L = (y - F)² → gradient = -(y - F) → negative gradient = y - F
# i.e. pseudo-residuals = ordinary residuals for regression

Step 3 — Fit a new tree to the pseudo-residuals

Train a decision tree (typically depth 3–8, not just stumps like AdaBoost) on the dataset {(xᵢ, rᵢ)}. The tree learns to predict the residuals — the direction in which predictions should move.

Step 4 — Update predictions with a learning rate

Add the tree's predictions to the current ensemble, scaled by η (learning rate):

F_k(x) = F_{k-1}(x) + η · h_k(x)

The learning rate shrinks each tree's contribution. A small η (0.01–0.1) means many trees are needed but generalisation is better. A large η is fast but overshoots and overfits.

Step 5 — Repeat until K trees are built

Go back to Step 2 using the updated F_k. Each round, the residuals get smaller. After K rounds, the final prediction is F_K(x) — the sum of all tree contributions plus the baseline.

Why "gradient" boosting?

Fitting a tree to pseudo-residuals is equivalent to one step of gradient descent — but in function space rather than parameter space. Instead of updating weights (like neural networks), we update the entire prediction function by adding a new tree. The learning rate η plays the same role as step size in regular gradient descent.

Key Hyperparameters

ParameterWhat it controlsEffect of increasingTypical range
n_estimatorsNumber of trees (boosting rounds)More rounds → lower bias, but risks overfitting100–1000
learning_rate (η)Shrinks each tree's contributionSlower learning — needs more trees but generalises better0.01–0.3
max_depthDepth of each treeMore complex trees — captures more interactions but overfits faster3–8
subsampleFraction of training rows per tree (stochastic GB)Lower = more randomness, less overfitting, like bagging0.5–1.0
min_samples_leafMinimum samples in a leaf nodeHigher = smoother trees, better regularisation1–20
Learning rate vs n_estimators trade-off: Always tune these together. Halving the learning rate usually means you need roughly double the trees for the same performance. Use early stopping (monitor validation loss) to find the right number of trees automatically.

Practical — sklearn GradientBoostingClassifier

The digits dataset has 1,797 samples of 8×8 pixel images of handwritten digits (0–9). We train a Gradient Boosting ensemble with 200 trees and watch accuracy grow at each boosting iteration.

Step 1 — Load data and train

from sklearn.model_selection import train_test_split
from sklearn.ensemble import GradientBoostingClassifier
import sklearn.datasets
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

data = sklearn.datasets.load_digits()

X_train, X_test, y_train, y_test = train_test_split(
    data['data'], data['target'], random_state=0
)

gbc = GradientBoostingClassifier(n_estimators=200)
gbc.fit(X_train, y_train)

Step 2 — Track accuracy at each boosting round

staged_predict() is a generator that yields predictions at each stage — very useful for finding the optimal number of trees without retraining.

# staged_predict() yields predictions after each individual tree is added
train_scores = [
    np.mean(np.equal(preds, y_train))
    for preds in gbc.staged_predict(X_train)
]
test_scores = [
    np.mean(np.equal(preds, y_test))
    for preds in gbc.staged_predict(X_test)
]

plt.plot(train_scores, label="Training")
plt.plot(test_scores,  label="Validation")
plt.legend(loc="lower right")
plt.xlabel("Boosting iteration")
plt.ylabel("Accuracy")

# Training accuracy → ~1.0; Validation peaks ~100 trees then plateaus
# The gap tells you if you're overfitting — use it to set n_estimators

Key insight

Training accuracy climbs to ~100% (boosting can memorise data). Validation accuracy peaks earlier then plateaus or drops — that peak is your optimal n_estimators. Always monitor validation loss to avoid over-boosting.

The Big Three Libraries

sklearn's implementation is clean but slow on large datasets. Three dedicated libraries dominate real-world use:

LibrarySpeedKey featuresBest for
XGBoostFastSparse data handling, column blocks for parallel splits, L1/L2 regularisation, out-of-core computationGeneral purpose, sparse features, competition ML
