L5: Autoregressive Models

Fitting AR and ARMA models on the NZ alcohol dataset — automatic lag selection, in-sample prediction, MAE evaluation, and implementing AR from scratch with sklearn.

Imports

import re
import pandas as pd
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

from statsmodels.api import tsa
from statsmodels.tsa.ar_model import ar_select_order
from statsmodels.tsa.arima.model import ARIMA
from sklearn.linear_model import LinearRegression
from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error
ImportWhat it provides
tsa.AutoRegStatsmodels AR model
ar_select_orderAutomatic lag selection via AIC/BIC
ARIMAARIMA and ARMA models
LinearRegressionUsed to implement AR manually with sklearn
mean_absolute_errorMeasures average prediction error

Step 1 — Load Data and Create Stationary Series

AR models require a stationary series. The alcohol dataset has quarterly seasonality — diff(4) removes it by subtracting the value from the same quarter one year ago.

alcohol_consumption = pd.read_csv(
    'data/NZAlcoholConsumption.csv',
    index_col='DATE'
)
alcohol_consumption.index = alcohol_consumption.index.map(parse_quarter)
alcohol_consumption.sort_index(inplace=True)

# Difference by 4 to remove quarterly seasonality, drop NaN rows
time_series = alcohol_consumption.TotalWine.diff(4).dropna()

plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
plt.plot(time_series, "-o")
plt.title("Does this look stationary?")
What to look for in the plot

The series should oscillate around a constant mean (roughly zero) with no upward/downward drift and no expanding variance. After diff(4) on TotalWine the trend is gone and the seasonal zigzag is removed — the result looks stationary enough to fit an AR model.

⚠️ Always visually inspect after differencing. If the plot still drifts or fans out, you may need an additional diff(1) on top of diff(4).

Step 2 — Set Explicit Frequency

Statsmodels requires the DatetimeIndex to have a known frequency before fitting. If you load data from CSV and map dates manually, the index has no frequency attached — statsmodels will refuse to fit.

# '3ME' = every 3 months, month-end (quarterly)
# method='ffill' fills any missing quarters by carrying the last value forward
time_series = time_series.asfreq('3ME', method='ffill')
Why this step is needed

Pandas DatetimeIndex can have timestamps without a declared frequency — it just knows the dates, not the spacing. Statsmodels uses the frequency to reason about lags in time terms. asfreq('3ME') declares "these points are spaced exactly 3 months apart" and creates any missing rows (filled with ffill). Without this, ar_select_order and AutoReg raise a frequency error.

Step 3 — Automatic Lag Selection with BIC

Instead of reading the PACF by eye, ar_select_order fits every AR model from p=1 to p=maxlag and scores each one. You pick the p with the lowest BIC.

# Fit AR(1) through AR(10), record AIC and BIC for each
set_of_models = ar_select_order(time_series, maxlag=10, old_names=False)

# set_of_models.bic is a dict: {(1,): value, (2,): value, ..., (4,): value}
# min(..., key=...) finds the key (lag tuple) with the lowest BIC value
# [-1] extracts the integer from the tuple, e.g. (4,) → 4
optlag = min(set_of_models.bic, key=set_of_models.bic.get)[-1]
print('Optimal p =', optlag)
Breaking down the BIC extraction

set_of_models.bic looks like {(1,): 45.2, (2,): 43.1, (4,): 41.3, ...} — keys are tuples of lag numbers, values are BIC scores.

min(..., key=set_of_models.bic.get) — finds the key whose value is smallest, i.e. the lag tuple with the best BIC.

[-1] — extracts the last element of the tuple. For (4,) that gives 4. For a model like (1, 4) it gives 4 — the largest lag in the model.

ScorePenalty per parameterTendency
AIC2Accepts slightly more complex models
BIClog(n) — grows with data sizePrefers simpler models, less overfitting risk

Step 4 — Fit the AR Model and Predict

# Build the model — nothing is fitted yet
ar = tsa.AutoReg(endog=time_series, lags=optlag)

# Fit: estimate the φ coefficients using least squares
ar_result = ar.fit()

# Predict in-sample — start=optlag skips the first p rows (no lag history yet)
prediction = ar_result.predict(start=optlag)

plt.figure(figsize=(12, 4))
plt.plot(time_series, '-o', label='true')
plt.plot(prediction, '-o', label='model')
plt.legend(fontsize=12)
Why start=optlag?

An AR(p) model needs p past values to make a prediction. Row 0 has no history. Row 1 has only 1 previous value. The first row with a full lag window is row p. Setting start=optlag skips straight to the first valid prediction — rows before it would be NaN anyway.

Evaluating with MAE

# Compare predictions against the true values from row optlag onwards
print('MAE = {0:.3f}'.format(mean_absolute_error(time_series[optlag:], prediction)))
What MAE tells you

Mean Absolute Error = average of |true − predicted| across all points. It's in the same units as your series. A MAE of 0.15 on a differenced wine series means the model is on average 0.15 units off per quarter. Lower is better. Compare against a naive baseline (e.g. "predict the previous value") to judge whether the AR model actually helps.

Inspecting the fitted model

# Full summary: coefficients, p-values, AIC, BIC, residual diagnostics
print(ar_result.summary())

Key things to read in the summary: coef — the φ values for each lag. P>|t| — if < 0.05, that lag is statistically significant. AIC/BIC — model quality. If some lags have p-value > 0.05, consider reducing p.

Step 5 — AR with Sklearn (Same Model, Different Tool)

AR is just linear regression where the features are lagged values of the target. You can implement it with sklearn by manually building the lag feature matrix.

The organize_data helper

def organize_data(to_forecast, window, horizon=1):
    """
    Turns a 1D time series into a supervised learning problem.
    Each row of X = one window of 'window' past values.
    Each value of y = the value 'horizon' steps ahead of that window.
    """
    shape = to_forecast.shape[:-1] + (to_forecast.shape[-1] - window + 1, window)
    strides = to_forecast.values.strides + (to_forecast.values.strides[-1],)
    X = np.lib.stride_tricks.as_strided(to_forecast, shape=shape, strides=strides)
    y = np.array([X[i+horizon][-1] for i in range(len(X)-horizon)])
    return X[:-horizon], y

X, y = organize_data(time_series, optlag)
What organize_data produces

For a series [a, b, c, d, e, f] with window=3:

X (features — 3 past values per row):
[a, b, c]
[b, c, d]
[c, d, e]

y (target — next value):
[d, e, f]

Each row of X is one "lookback window". LinearRegression then learns how to combine the 3 values in each window to predict the next one — which is exactly what AR does.

Fit LinearRegression as AR

lr = LinearRegression()
lr.fit(X, y)
prediction = lr.predict(X)

plt.figure(figsize=(12, 4))
plt.plot(time_series.values, '-o')
plt.plot(np.arange(optlag, len(time_series)), prediction, '-o', label='prediction')
plt.legend(fontsize=12)
The result is identical to AutoReg

Both methods fit the same mathematical model — they minimise the same least squares objective. The sklearn approach gives you the same predictions and coefficients. The advantage of the sklearn route: you can swap LinearRegression for any other sklearn regressor (Ridge, GradientBoosting, etc.) to build a custom autoregressive model with different assumptions.

ARMA — Adding Past Errors as Features

In an AR model the residuals e(t) are assumed to be pure noise — discarded. But what if those errors carry information? ARMA adds the past errors as additional predictors.

The ARMA formula

AR part: φ₁·y(t−1) + φ₂·y(t−2) + ... + φₚ·y(t−p)
MA part: θ₁·e(t−1) + θ₂·e(t−2) + ... + θ_q·e(t−q)

Full: y(t) = c + AR(p) + MA(q) + e(t)

The AR part uses past values. The MA part uses past forecast errors. Together they model both the memory of the series and the memory of the mistakes.

Fitting ARMA in statsmodels

# ARIMA(order=(p, d, q)) — setting d=0 turns ARIMA into ARMA
# d is the differencing order — we already differenced manually so d=0
arma = ARIMA(time_series, order=(3, 0, 3))
arma_result = arma.fit()

# start=3 because p=3 — same reason as AR: need 3 past values first
prediction = arma_result.predict(start=3)

plt.figure(figsize=(12, 4))
plt.plot(time_series, '-o', label='true')
plt.plot(prediction, '-o', label='model')
plt.legend()

print('MAE = {0:.3f}'.format(mean_absolute_error(time_series[3:], prediction)))
Why ARIMA with d=0 is ARMA

ARIMA stands for AutoRegressive Integrated Moving Average. The I (Integrated) part is the differencing step — d tells the model how many times to difference. Setting d=0 means "don't difference" — the model reduces to plain ARMA. Since we already applied diff(4) manually before fitting, we pass d=0.

ModelorderWhat it uses
AR(p)(p, 0, 0)Past values only
MA(q)(0, 0, q)Past errors only
ARMA(p,q)(p, 0, q)Past values + past errors
ARIMA(p,d,q)(p, d, q)ARMA + built-in differencing

Full Workflow Summary

StepCodePurpose
1. Stationarise.diff(4).dropna()Remove seasonality so AR assumptions hold
2. Set frequency.asfreq('3ME', method='ffill')Tell statsmodels the time spacing
3. Select par_select_order(...).bicFind optimal lag via BIC
4. Fit ARAutoReg(series, lags=p).fit()Learn the φ coefficients
5. Predict.predict(start=p)In-sample fitted values
6. Evaluatemean_absolute_error(...)Average prediction error
7. ExtendARIMA(series, order=(p,0,q))Add MA terms if residuals have structure

🧪 Quick Check

Time Series Analysis

L6: Time Series Testing

Residual diagnostics, genuine forecasting on held-out data, automatic ARMA selection, and cross-validation for time series — using Google Trends climate change data.

Why Testing Matters

Building a model is only half the job. A model that looks good on the data it was trained on might be completely useless on new data — or worse, confidently wrong. Testing is how you find out before it matters.

For time series specifically, testing is harder than in standard ML because:

  • You cannot shuffle the data. Rows are not independent — the order is the information. A random split contaminates the future into training.
  • In-sample fit is not the same as forecast accuracy. A model that perfectly explains past data may fail to predict the future if it overfit to historical noise.
  • The model can look complete but still be wrong. Even if predictions visually track the series, the residuals may reveal hidden structure the model missed.

Testing for time series therefore involves two distinct questions:

Question 1 — Did the model capture everything it could from the training data?

Answered by residual diagnostics: check that what the model got wrong (the residuals) is truly random — no mean bias, no autocorrelation, no growing variance. If residuals have structure, the model is incomplete and could be improved.

Question 2 — Can the model actually forecast unseen future data?

Answered by held-out test evaluation: train on the past, predict the future, measure the error on data the model never saw. This is the only honest measure of forecast accuracy.

Important Things to Know Before You Start

1. In-sample fit ≠ forecast accuracy

When you call .predict(start=optlag) on a fitted model, you get in-sample predictions — the model is predicting data it already trained on. This always looks better than real forecasting. It tells you how well the model describes the past, not how well it predicts the future. Always evaluate on a separate test set.

2. The split must be chronological

Training set = all points before a cutoff. Test set = all points after. Never random. If a future point leaks into training, the model can "remember" it — your test MAE will look great but the model is useless in deployment.

3. Forecasting the future is genuinely hard

Simple models like AR and ARMA capture structure well but don't perform miracles on real-world data. A high test MAE doesn't mean the model is broken — it might mean the series has genuine unpredictability. The right question is: does this model beat a simple baseline? (e.g. "always predict the last known value"). If yes, the model is adding value.

4. Residual checks and test evaluation answer different questions

Residual diagnosticsTest set evaluation
What it checksIs the model structurally complete?Can the model forecast unseen data?
Data usedTraining data (in-sample)Held-out future data (out-of-sample)
ToolsMean residual, ACF plot, Shapiro-WilkMAE, plot predictions vs test
When to do itAfter fitting, before forecastingAfter fitting, on genuinely future data

You need both. A model can pass all residual checks (complete on training data) and still forecast poorly (overfit). And a model with poor residuals should be improved before trusting its forecasts at all.

Dataset — Google Trends

This practical uses weekly Google search interest in "climate change" worldwide — a real-world series with trend and noise. The CSV has 2 header rows, so skiprows=2 is needed.

# skiprows=2 skips the two header rows pandas can't auto-parse
df = pd.read_csv('data/climate_change.csv', skiprows=2)

# Convert Week column to proper datetime objects
df['Week'] = pd.to_datetime(df['Week'])
df.set_index('Week', inplace=True)

# Select just the one column to get a Series
time_series = df['climate change: (Worldwide)']

# Attach inferred frequency to avoid statsmodels warnings
time_series.index = pd.DatetimeIndex(
    time_series.index.values,
    freq=time_series.index.inferred_freq
)
Why attach a frequency?

When you build an index manually from a CSV, pandas knows the dates but not the spacing. inferred_freq looks at the gaps between dates and guesses the pattern (e.g. "W-SUN" for weekly). Assigning it back tells statsmodels the series is weekly — required for lag-aware operations to work without warnings.

Part 1 — Fit AR Model Using AIC

Same workflow as L5 but using AIC instead of BIC for lag selection, and extracting optlag from mod.ar_lags instead of the BIC dictionary.

# ic="aic" — slightly more permissive than BIC, accepts larger p if fit justifies it
mod = ar_select_order(time_series, maxlag=10, ic="aic")

# mod.ar_lags is a tuple of selected lag indices, e.g. (1, 3) or (2,)
# max() picks the largest lag number — this becomes p
optlag = max(mod.ar_lags)
print('Optimal p =', optlag)

ar = tsa.AutoReg(time_series, lags=optlag)
ar_result = ar.fit()
prediction = ar_result.predict(start=optlag)

plt.figure(figsize=(12, 4))
plt.plot(time_series, '-o', label='true')
plt.plot(prediction, '-o', label='model')
plt.legend(fontsize=12)
max(mod.ar_lags) vs the L5 BIC approach

mod.ar_lags contains the lag indices that the selection algorithm included in the best model — e.g. (1, 3) means lags 1 and 3 are both significant. max() gives you the highest lag, which is the order p you pass to AutoReg.

In L5 we used min(set_of_models.bic, key=...) to find the best BIC key. Both approaches produce the same integer p — just read from different attributes of the result.

Part 2 — What Are Residuals and Why Do They Matter?

A residual is simply the difference between what the model predicted and what actually happened at each point:

The residual formula

e(t) = y(t) − ŷ(t)

y(t) — the actual observed value
ŷ(t) — what the model predicted
e(t) — the leftover error (the residual)

If the model has captured all the structure in the data, the residuals should be pure noise — random, unpredictable, with no pattern. If there is still a pattern in the residuals, it means the model missed something it could have used to make better predictions. Residual diagnostics are how you verify the model is actually complete.

# time_series[optlag:] aligns true values with the predictions (same length)
# .values.flatten() converts to a plain numpy array for arithmetic
fit_residuals = time_series[optlag:].values.flatten() - prediction

Part 2a — Property 1: Zero Mean

What it means: the average of all residuals should be approximately zero — the model isn't consistently over-predicting or under-predicting.

Why it matters: if the mean residual is, say, +5, it means the model is on average 5 units below the true value at every step. That's a systematic bias you could fix by simply adding 5 to every forecast. A non-zero mean is the easiest problem to fix — but it tells you the model is fundamentally miscalibrated.

print("Mean residual:", np.mean(fit_residuals))
# Want: a number very close to zero, e.g. 0.002 or -0.001
# Bad:  a number like 4.7 or -3.2 — model has a bias

plt.plot(fit_residuals)
plt.axhline(0, color='red', linestyle='--')
plt.title("Residuals should scatter randomly around zero")
plt.show()
What to look for in the residual plot

Good: random scatter above and below the red zero line, no overall direction.

Drifting upward: model is consistently under-predicting — the mean isn't zero.

Funnel shape (spread grows over time): the variance isn't constant — the model is less accurate in some periods than others (called heteroscedasticity).

Repeating zigzag: there's a seasonal or cyclical pattern the model missed entirely.

Part 2b — Property 2: Uncorrelated Residuals (ACF)

What it means: knowing the error at time t should tell you nothing about the error at time t+1, t+2, etc. The residuals should have no memory of each other.

Why it matters: if consecutive residuals are correlated — e.g. every time the model under-predicts this week it also under-predicts next week — then the past errors contain information about future errors. That means there's structure left in the data the model could have captured but didn't. Correlated residuals = incomplete model.

What ACF measures on residuals

You already know ACF from preprocessing — it measures how correlated a series is with a lagged version of itself. When applied to residuals, it answers: "does the error at time t predict the error k steps later?"

If ACF(lag=1) is high, last period's mistake predicts this period's mistake — there's a lag-1 pattern in the errors. If ACF(lag=4) is high, there's a quarterly pattern in the errors. Either way the model missed it.

from statsmodels.graphics.tsaplots import plot_acf

plot_acf(fit_residuals, lags=10)
plt.title("ACF of residuals — should show no significant spikes")
plt.show()
How to read the ACF plot of residuals

The plot shows a vertical bar at each lag. The blue shaded region is the 95% confidence band — bars inside it are not statistically different from zero.

All bars inside the band → residuals are white noise → model is complete ✓

Spike at lag 1 → the model missed a lag-1 dependency → increase p by 1

Spike at lag 4 → quarterly pattern left unexplained → add AR(4) term or use ARMA

Many spikes → model is fundamentally missing structure → try ARMA or revisit stationarity

This is the most important residual check. A biased mean can be corrected trivially; leftover autocorrelation means the model is structurally wrong.

Part 2c — Property 3: Constant Variance

What it means: the size of the errors should be roughly the same throughout the series — not small early on and large later, or vice versa.

Why it matters: if the errors grow over time (a funnel shape), the model is less reliable for certain periods. This is called heteroscedasticity. It doesn't bias the forecasts but it makes your error estimates unreliable — you can't trust that the MAE you measured on one period applies to another.

How to check: just look at the residual plot. The vertical spread of the scatter should be roughly the same from left to right. No funnel, no dramatic increase in range.

Fix if violated: apply a log-transform to the original series before fitting. Log compression squashes large values, which often stabilises variance.

Part 2d — Property 4: Normally Distributed Residuals

What it means: the errors should follow a bell curve — most errors small and near zero, fewer large errors, roughly symmetric on both sides.

Why it matters: normality of residuals is needed if you want to build prediction intervals — the confidence bands around a forecast (e.g. "I predict 75 ± 8"). The maths behind those intervals assumes the errors follow a normal distribution. If they don't, the intervals will be wrong widths. For the point forecasts themselves, normality doesn't matter.

Visual check — histogram

plt.hist(fit_residuals, bins=15)
plt.title("Should look like a bell curve centred at zero")
plt.show()

Look for a roughly symmetric hump centred at zero. Heavy tails, a skewed shape, or two humps all suggest non-normality.

Formal check — Shapiro-Wilk test

The histogram is subjective — the Shapiro-Wilk test gives you a statistical answer.

What it does: tests whether a dataset could plausibly have been drawn from a normal distribution, by comparing the actual data to what a perfect normal distribution of the same size would look like.

The null hypothesis H₀: the data is normally distributed.

from scipy.stats import shapiro

test = shapiro(fit_residuals)
print("p-value:", test.pvalue)
How to interpret the p-value

p > 0.05 — fail to reject H₀ → the data is consistent with a normal distribution → residuals are approximately normal ✓

p < 0.05 — reject H₀ → the data is unlikely to have come from a normal distribution → residuals are not normal

Remember: the null hypothesis here is normality (the thing you want to be true). So a high p-value is the good outcome — unlike the ADF test where a low p-value was good. Always check which direction a test points before interpreting.

⚠️ If residuals fail the Shapiro-Wilk test, try a log-transform on the original series before fitting: time_series = np.log(time_series). This often normalises residuals for data with multiplicative growth (like revenue or search interest).
PropertyWhy it mattersHow to checkWhat you want
Zero meanNo systematic bias in forecastsnp.mean(residuals)≈ 0
UncorrelatedNo exploitable structure left in errorsACF plot of residualsAll lags inside blue band
Constant varianceError size is consistent across timeResidual plot shapeEven spread, no funnel
Normal distributionPrediction intervals are validHistogram + Shapiro-WilkBell curve, p > 0.05

Part 3 — Genuine Forecasting on a Test Set

In-sample predictions show how well the model fits data it already saw. To test if it can actually forecast, you need genuinely unseen future data.

The time series train/test rule

Always split chronologically — train on the past, test on the future. Never shuffle or randomly sample. Using future data in training is data leakage and will make the model look far better than it really is.

AR forecast on held-out test set

# Hold out the last 40 points as test — always the END of the series
train = time_series[:-40]
test  = time_series[-40:]

# Select lag using BIC on training data only
mod = ar_select_order(train, maxlag=10, ic="bic")
optlag = max(mod.ar_lags)

# Fit on training data only
ar = tsa.AutoReg(train, lags=optlag)
arfit = ar.fit()

# Predict up to the end of the full series, then slice the last 40
prediction = arfit.predict(end=time_series.index[-1])[-len(test):]

plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
plt.plot(test, '-o', label='test data')
plt.plot(prediction, '--o', label='prediction')
plt.legend(fontsize=12)

print('AR MAE:', mean_absolute_error(test, prediction))
Why predict(end=...) then slice [-len(test):]?

arfit.predict(end=time_series.index[-1]) generates predictions from where the training data ends all the way to the last date in the full series. Slicing [-len(test):] keeps only the last 40 predictions — the ones that correspond to the test period. This is cleaner than computing exact start/end indices manually.

Part 4 — ARMA with Automatic p and q Selection

For AR we only needed to find p. For ARMA we need both p and q. arma_order_select_ic tries every combination up to max_ar and max_ma and picks the best one.

# Try all combinations of p ∈ [0,2] and q ∈ [0,2], score with BIC
param_choice = tsa.arma_order_select_ic(
    train, max_ar=2, max_ma=2, ic='bic', trend='c'
)
p, q = param_choice['bic_min_order']
print('Best ARMA(p,q) =', p, q)

# d=0 — already stationary, no built-in differencing needed
arma = tsa.ARIMA(train, order=(p, 0, q))
arma_fit = arma.fit()

prediction = arma_fit.predict(end=time_series.index[-1])[-len(test):]

plt.figure(figsize=(8, 6))
plt.plot(test, '-o', label='test data')
plt.plot(prediction, '--o', label='ARMA prediction')
plt.legend(fontsize=12)

print('ARMA MAE:', mean_absolute_error(test, prediction))
What param_choice['bic_min_order'] gives you

arma_order_select_ic returns a results object. bic_min_order is a tuple (p, q) — the combination of AR order and MA order that had the lowest BIC across all combinations tried. If bic_min_order is (1, 0), the best ARMA is actually just AR(1) — the MA terms added nothing.

⚠️ If the ARMA MAE is lower than the AR MAE, the past errors contained information the AR model was ignoring. If it's similar or worse, the series is well described by AR alone and the MA terms are noise.

Part 5 — Cross-Validation for Time Series

A single train/test split gives one MAE number which may be lucky or unlucky depending on which 40 points ended up in the test set. Cross-validation gives a more robust estimate by averaging over many splits.

Forward chaining — the only valid CV for time series

Standard k-fold randomly assigns rows to folds — invalid for time series because future data would leak into training. Forward chaining always trains on the past and tests on the future:

Fold 1: train [1..30], test [31]
Fold 2: train [1..31], test [32]
Fold 3: train [1..32], test [33]
...

Each fold expands the training window by one point. Every test point is genuinely unseen at the time of prediction.

Cross-validation function

def Forecasting_CV(time_series, training_size, optlag):
    train = time_series[:training_size]
    test  = time_series[training_size:]

    ar = tsa.AutoReg(train, lags=optlag)
    ar_result = ar.fit()

    # Predict from end of train to end of full series, keep test portion
    prediction = ar_result.predict(end=len(time_series)-1)[-len(test):]

    return mean_absolute_error(time_series.values[training_size:], prediction)

Loop over all training sizes

mae_errors = []

# Minimum 30 training points; stop 30 from the end to always have a test set
for training_size in range(30, len(time_series) - 30):
    mod = ar_select_order(time_series[:training_size], maxlag=10, ic="aic")
    optlag = max(mod.ar_lags)
    mae = Forecasting_CV(time_series, training_size, optlag)
    mae_errors.append(mae)

print("Average MAE across all folds:", np.mean(mae_errors))
Why average MAE is more meaningful than a single split

A single test split evaluates the model on one specific future window. If that window happened to be unusually volatile or calm, the MAE is misleading. Averaging across hundreds of different training sizes gives a stable estimate of how the model performs across the full range of forecast horizons and data regimes. It also reveals whether accuracy degrades as the horizon grows.

Quick single-run example

# Train on 85% of the data, test on the remaining 15%
Forecasting_crossValidation(time_series, int(len(time_series) * 0.85), optlag=3)

Full Workflow Summary

StepCodePurpose
Load datapd.read_csv(skiprows=2)Handle non-standard CSV headers
Set frequency.index.inferred_freqAvoid statsmodels warnings
Select lag (AIC)ar_select_order(ic="aic")Auto-select p from training data
Fit ARAutoReg(train, lags=p).fit()Estimate φ coefficients
Check residualsMean, ACF plot, Shapiro-WilkConfirm model is complete
Forecast.predict(end=...)[-len(test):]Genuinely unseen future points
Select ARMAarma_order_select_ic(max_ar, max_ma)Auto-select both p and q
Cross-validateForward chaining loopStable MAE across all horizons

🧪 Quick Check

Time Series Analysis

L7: Exponential Smoothing

A family of forecasting methods that weight recent observations more heavily — from simple smoothing to handling trends and seasonality — without requiring stationarity.

Why Exponential Smoothing?

Everything we've built so far — AR, ARMA, ARIMA — requires you to first make the series stationary. That means differencing, testing, possibly differencing again. It's a multi-step pipeline before you even start modelling.

Exponential smoothing takes a different approach. It doesn't assume stationarity. It doesn't require you to know the structure of the series upfront. It just asks: what weights should we give to past observations, and how quickly should those weights decay?

The key insight

Not all past observations are equally useful. What happened last week is more relevant than what happened two years ago. Exponential smoothing formalises this by giving each past observation a weight that decays exponentially the further back you go — recent data counts more, old data fades out automatically.

Why use it over AR/ARIMA?

  • No stationarity required. You can apply it directly to raw data with trend and seasonality.
  • Simpler to interpret. The smoothing parameter α has a direct, intuitive meaning.
  • Competitive in practice. Despite their simplicity, ES methods consistently beat more complex models on real benchmark datasets.
  • Three variants cover most situations. No trend, trend only, or trend plus seasonality — pick the one that matches your data.

Important Things to Know Before You Start

1. The α parameter controls memory

Every exponential smoothing model has a smoothing parameter α (between 0 and 1). It controls how quickly old observations are forgotten:

α valueEffectUse when
α close to 1Almost all weight on the most recent point — fast to react, noisySeries changes rapidly, recent past is most predictive
α close to 0Weight spread across many past points — slow to react, smoothSeries is stable, long memory, or very noisy
α ≈ 0.1–0.3Moderate smoothing — typical range in practiceMost real-world series

In practice you don't choose α manually — statsmodels optimises it from the data by minimising the sum of squared forecast errors.

2. Three models — pick based on your data

ModelAlso calledHandlesUse when
SESSimple Exp. SmoothingLevel onlyNo trend, no seasonality
DES / Holt'sDouble Exp. SmoothingLevel + trendTrend present, no seasonality
TES / Holt-WintersTriple Exp. SmoothingLevel + trend + seasonalityBoth trend and seasonality

3. SES produces a flat forecast

This is the most common surprise. SES models the level of the series — a smoothed estimate of where the series currently sits. When you ask it to forecast into the future, it has no information about direction, so it projects that level forward as a flat horizontal line. This is correct behaviour — not a bug. If your data has a trend, SES is simply the wrong model.

4. Seasonality needs a period

Holt-Winters requires you to specify seasonal_periods — how many time steps make up one full seasonal cycle. You find this from the ACF plot: the lag with the largest significant spike is your season length. For monthly data with annual seasonality it's 12. For weekly data with 6-monthly patterns it's 26.

From Naive to Exponential — The Motivation

Before jumping to ES, it helps to see the two naive extremes and why neither is satisfying.

Naive Method 1 — Always predict the last value

# Predict the last known value for every future step
h = 50
forecast_dates = pd.date_range(series.index[-1], periods=h, freq="B")
naive_forecast = [series[-1]] * h  # repeat the last value h times

Too much weight on one single point. One noisy day throws off all future predictions. The forecast is a flat line at that one value.

Naive Method 2 — Always predict the overall mean

naive_forecast = [series.mean()] * h  # same for all future steps

Treats a reading from 5 years ago as equally important as last week. Completely ignores recent behaviour. The forecast is a flat line at the long-run average.

Exponential smoothing is the middle ground

Neither "only the last point" nor "equal weight to everything". Instead: give the most weight to the most recent observation, and let weights decay exponentially as you go back in time.

ŷ(T+1) = α·y(T) + α(1−α)·y(T−1) + α(1−α)²·y(T−2) + ...

The weight on observation k steps back is α(1−α)ᵏ. Since 0 < (1−α) < 1, this gets smaller and smaller the further back you go. The total of all weights sums to 1.

Dataset and Loading

This practical uses monthly company revenue data. Revenue is in raw units so we divide by 1 million for readability.

import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
from statsmodels.tsa.api import ExponentialSmoothing, SimpleExpSmoothing
from statsmodels.graphics.tsaplots import plot_acf

full_df = pd.read_csv("data/MonthlySales.csv")

# Convert revenue to millions for readability
full_df["Revenue"] = full_df["Revenue"].apply(lambda x: x / 1e6)

# Keep only the two relevant columns
df = full_df.loc[:, ["Period", "Revenue"]].copy()
df.dropna(inplace=True)

# Parse dates and set as index
df["Period"] = pd.to_datetime(df["Period"], format="%d.%m.%Y")
df.set_index("Period", inplace=True)

# Attach inferred frequency (avoids statsmodels warnings)
df.index = pd.DatetimeIndex(df.index.values, freq=df.index.inferred_freq)

# Train/test split — last 12 months as test (1 year)
train = df[:-12]
test  = df[-12:]
Why format="%d.%m.%Y"?

The dates in this CSV are stored as 31.01.2018 — day.month.year with dots. Pandas can't auto-detect this format so you must specify it explicitly. %d=day, %m=month, %Y=4-digit year. Without the format string, pandas either fails or mis-parses the dates.

Simple Exponential Smoothing (SES)

SES is the foundation. It models only the level of the series — a smoothed estimate of where the series currently is. It works well when there is no trend and no seasonality. On revenue data with a clear upward trend, we expect it to struggle — and seeing that failure is exactly what motivates the next model.

# initialization_method="estimated" — let statsmodels optimise α and the
# initial level l₀ from the data, rather than setting them manually
ses_model = SimpleExpSmoothing(train, initialization_method="estimated").fit()

# forecast(n) — generate n steps into the future
y_pred = ses_model.forecast(len(test))

plt.figure()
plt.plot(df, label="true data")
plt.plot(test.index, y_pred, label="prediction")
plt.plot(test, label="test data")
plt.legend()
Why does SES produce a flat forecast?

SES only tracks the level — where the series currently is. It has no concept of direction (trend). When asked to predict the future, it has nothing to go on except the current level, so it projects that level forward as a flat horizontal line. This is not a bug — it's what SES is designed to do. The flat line says: "based on recent history, my best estimate is that the series will stay at this level." On a trending series, this is clearly wrong, which tells us we need the next model.

Holt's Linear Model — Double Exponential Smoothing (DES)

Holt's method extends SES by adding a second component: the trend. Now the model tracks two things simultaneously — the current level and the current direction of change. This lets it project a sloped line into the future instead of a flat one.

How DES works

DES uses two smoothing equations — one for the level (like SES) and one for the trend. The trend equation tracks how fast the level is changing, with its own smoothing parameter β. The forecast is: ŷ(T+h) = level + h × trend — the current level plus h steps of the current trend.

# trend="add" — additive trend: the trend is added to the level each step
# initialization_method="estimated" — optimise all parameters from data
des_model = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train,
    trend="add",
    initialization_method="estimated"
).fit()

y_pred = des_model.forecast(len(test))

plt.figure()
plt.plot(df, label="true data")
plt.plot(test.index, y_pred, label="prediction")
plt.plot(test, label="test data")
plt.legend()

Manual vs automatic parameters

# Option A — let statsmodels optimise everything
des_auto = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train, trend="add", initialization_method="estimated"
).fit()

# Option B — specify parameters manually (useful for experimenting)
des_manual = ExponentialSmoothing(train, trend="add").fit(
    smoothing_level=0.1,   # α — controls level smoothing
    smoothing_trend=0.5    # β — controls trend smoothing
)
When manual parameters beat auto

Sometimes manually tuned parameters outperform the optimiser, especially on short datasets where the optimiser has little data to work with. The optimiser minimises in-sample error — it doesn't know about your test set. If you know the series is slowly evolving, a small β (e.g. 0.1) will prevent the trend from chasing noise. Always compare both on a validation set.

Damped trend

# damped_trend=True — trend effect gradually weakens over the forecast horizon
# Useful for long-range forecasts where you don't trust the trend to continue indefinitely
des_damped = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train, trend="add", damped_trend=True, initialization_method="estimated"
).fit()

A damped trend is a safety net for long-horizon forecasting. It says: "the trend is real now, but I don't trust it to continue at the same rate forever." The forecast starts sloped then gradually flattens out.

Holt-Winters — Triple Exponential Smoothing (TES)

Holt-Winters adds a third component — seasonality. It tracks the level, the trend, and a seasonal index for each period within the cycle. This makes it the most powerful of the three models and the right choice when the series has a repeating pattern on top of a trend.

Step 1 — Find the seasonal period using ACF

Before fitting, you need to know how long one seasonal cycle is. The ACF plot tells you — the largest significant spike at a lag greater than 1 is your season length.

# Plot ACF to find the dominant seasonal lag
plot_acf(df, lags=15)
plt.show()
# Look for the largest spike — e.g. lag 6 means a 6-month cycle
# Use that value as seasonal_periods

Step 2 — Fit Holt-Winters

tes_model = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train,
    trend="add",           # include additive trend component
    seasonal="add",        # include additive seasonal component
    seasonal_periods=6,    # one cycle = 6 months (from ACF)
    initialization_method="estimated"
).fit()

y_pred = tes_model.forecast(len(test))

plt.figure()
plt.plot(df, label="true data")
plt.plot(test.index, y_pred, label="Holt-Winters prediction")
plt.plot(test, label="test data")
plt.legend()
Additive vs multiplicative seasonality

Additive (seasonal="add"): the seasonal fluctuation is a fixed amount added to the level — e.g. revenue always goes up by £2M in December regardless of the level. Use when seasonal swings are roughly the same size throughout the series.

Multiplicative (seasonal="mul"): the seasonal fluctuation is a percentage of the level — e.g. revenue is always 20% higher in December. Use when seasonal swings grow proportionally as the series level grows. Common in revenue and sales data.

ParameterOptionsWhen to use
trend"add", "mul", None"add" for linear trend; None for SES
seasonal"add", "mul", None"add" for constant swings; "mul" for growing swings
seasonal_periodsintegerFrom ACF — the dominant lag
damped_trendTrue, FalseTrue for long-horizon forecasts
initialization_method"estimated"Always — lets statsmodels optimise all parameters

Comparing All Three Models

from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error

ses = SimpleExpSmoothing(train, initialization_method="estimated").fit()
des = ExponentialSmoothing(train, trend="add", initialization_method="estimated").fit()
tes = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train, trend="add", seasonal="add",
    seasonal_periods=6, initialization_method="estimated"
).fit()

ses_pred = ses.forecast(len(test))
des_pred = des.forecast(len(test))
tes_pred = tes.forecast(len(test))

print("SES MAE:", mean_absolute_error(test, ses_pred))
print("DES MAE:", mean_absolute_error(test, des_pred))
print("TES MAE:", mean_absolute_error(test, tes_pred))
How to interpret the MAE comparison

If SES MAE > DES MAE: the series has a trend that SES couldn't capture — DES was the right upgrade.

If DES MAE > TES MAE: the series also has seasonality — TES was worth adding.

If TES MAE ≈ DES MAE: the seasonality component isn't helping — maybe you chose the wrong seasonal_periods, or the series doesn't actually have seasonality. Check the ACF again.

Full Workflow Summary

StepWhat to doWhy
1. Plot the seriesLook for trend and seasonalityDetermines which model to use
2. Split train/testtrain=df[:-12], test=df[-12:]Chronological split — never random
3. Fit SESSimpleExpSmoothing(train).fit()Baseline — sees flat forecast if trend exists
4. Fit DESExponentialSmoothing(train, trend="add").fit()Handles trend — better if data drifts
5. Find seasonal periodplot_acf(df, lags=15) — read largest spikeRequired input for Holt-Winters
6. Fit TESExponentialSmoothing(train, trend="add", seasonal="add", seasonal_periods=k).fit()Handles trend + seasonality
7. Compare MAEmean_absolute_error(test, model.forecast(...))Pick the model that forecasts best

🧪 Quick Check

Time Series Analysis

L8: Prophet

Facebook's open-source forecasting library — decompose time series into trend, seasonality, and one-off events, then forecast with minimal preprocessing.

Why Prophet?

Every model we've built so far — AR, ARMA, ARIMA, Exponential Smoothing — requires you to understand and handle the structure of the series yourself: test for stationarity, difference to remove trend, identify seasonal periods, choose model orders. Prophet flips this around.

Prophet's goal: make accurate forecasting accessible to analysts who are not time series experts, on real business data that has multiple overlapping patterns and irregular one-off events.

What makes Prophet different

AR/ARIMA: predicts y(t) from past values y(t−1), y(t−2)... The series must be stationary. You manage stationarity yourself.

Prophet: predicts y(t) from time directly — it models growth, seasonality and events as explicit, interpretable components and adds them together. No stationarity requirement. No differencing. No lag selection.

When to choose Prophet over other models

  • Multiple overlapping seasonalities — e.g. weekly patterns on top of yearly patterns.
  • Known one-off events — holidays, strikes, product launches — you can tell the model about them explicitly.
  • Trend changepoints — the growth rate changed at some point in history; Prophet detects where.
  • Missing data or irregular timestamps — Prophet handles gaps natively, unlike ARIMA which needs a regular index.
  • Long-range forecasting — weeks to years ahead, not just the next few steps.

Important Things to Know Before You Start

1. The additive model — how Prophet works

Prophet decomposes the series into three components that are added together:

y(t) = g(t) + s(t) + h(t) + ε(t)

g(t) — trend: the long-term growth direction, with changepoints where the rate shifts
s(t) — seasonality: repeating patterns (weekly, yearly) modelled using Fourier series
h(t) — holidays / one-off events: special days that push values up or down
ε(t) — noise: what's left over

Each component is estimated separately and is directly interpretable. You can plot them individually and ask: "how much does the summer contribute?" or "how much does a bank holiday suppress demand?"

2. Prophet requires columns named "ds" and "y"

This is non-negotiable. Prophet will not accept any other column names. ds must contain the dates (as datetime objects), y must contain the values to forecast. If your DataFrame has different column names you must rename them first.

3. Seasonality is controlled by Fourier order

Prophet models seasonal patterns using a Fourier series — a sum of sine and cosine waves. The Fourier order (set via weekly_seasonality, yearly_seasonality) controls how many waves are summed:

OrderEffectRisk
Low (e.g. 1)Very smooth seasonal curve — misses sharp weekly/yearly patternsUnderfitting
Default (3–10)Good fit to typical seasonal shapesUsually safe
High (e.g. 20)Wiggly curve that fits training data tightlyOverfitting — forecasts will be unstable

4. Changepoints are where the trend rate changes

Real-world series rarely grow at a constant rate. Prophet automatically detects changepoints — moments in history where the trend shifted significantly (e.g. a new product launch caused faster growth, or a recession slowed it). You can visualise them to understand turning points in your data.

Dataset and Loading

TFL daily Santander bike hire counts in London — approximately 7 years of daily data. The series has clear yearly seasonality (summer peaks, winter dips), a slight upward trend, and notable outliers around the 2015 tube strikes.

from prophet import Prophet
import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Read from Excel, select only the two relevant columns
df_data = pd.read_excel("data/tfl-daily-cycle-hires.xls", sheet_name="Data")
df_data = df_data[["Day", "Number of Bicycle Hires"]]

# Convert datetime to date (Prophet expects dates, not full timestamps)
df_data["Day"] = df_data["Day"].dt.date

# Prophet requires exactly these column names — rename before doing anything else
df_data = df_data.rename(columns={
    "Day": "ds",
    "Number of Bicycle Hires": "y"
})

df_data.head()
Explore before modelling

Always plot and check a histogram first. For this dataset:
Time series plot: reveals seasonal rise/fall, upward trend, and outlier spike around 2015 strikes.
Histogram: roughly Gaussian — no need for a log transform. The 2015 outliers are visible as a long right tail.

plt.plot(df_data["ds"], df_data["y"])
plt.xlabel("Date")
plt.ylabel("Number of cycle hires")
plt.show()

plt.hist(df_data["y"], bins=50)
plt.show()

Adding Special Events — Holidays and Strikes

Prophet's biggest practical advantage is the ability to tell the model about specific days that break the normal pattern. Without this, the model would treat the 2015 tube strike spike as a random outlier and try to "explain" it with trend or seasonality — corrupting those components.

Special events are passed as a DataFrame with exactly two columns: holiday (a name string) and ds (the dates).

Bank holidays

# Load bank holiday dates from CSV, convert to datetime
dates = pd.to_datetime(list(pd.read_csv("data/BankHolidayLists.csv")["Date"]))
bank_holidays = pd.DataFrame({"holiday": "BankHoliday", "ds": dates})

Tube strike days

# Manually specify known strike dates
dates = pd.to_datetime([
    "2017-08-05", "2017-08-06", "2017-02-06",
    "2015-07-09", "2015-07-08", "2015-03-08"
])
strike_days = pd.DataFrame({"holiday": "strike", "ds": dates})

Combine into one DataFrame

# pd.concat stacks the two DataFrames vertically
all_holidays_strikes = pd.concat((bank_holidays, strike_days))
# Convert ds to plain date (no time component) for consistency
all_holidays_strikes["ds"] = all_holidays_strikes["ds"].dt.date
Why separate bank holidays from strikes?

By giving each event type a different holiday name, Prophet learns a separate effect size for each type. Bank holidays suppress bike hires (people don't commute). Tube strikes boost them (commuters switch to bikes). If you gave them the same name, Prophet would average the two effects into one, getting both wrong.

Fitting the Model and Forecasting

# Create model — pass the holidays DataFrame so Prophet knows about special days
forecast_model = Prophet(holidays=all_holidays_strikes)

# Fit on the full historical data
forecast_model.fit(df_data)

# Create a DataFrame of future dates — 365 days ahead, including past dates
# include_history=True means the DataFrame covers both past and future
df_dates = forecast_model.make_future_dataframe(periods=365, include_history=True)

# Generate predictions — returns a DataFrame with yhat (forecast),
# yhat_lower and yhat_upper (uncertainty interval), plus each component
model_predictions = forecast_model.predict(df_dates)

# Built-in plot: black dots = observed, blue line = forecast, shaded = uncertainty
forecast_model.plot(model_predictions)
plt.show()
The predict() output columns

yhat — the point forecast
yhat_lower, yhat_upper — 80% uncertainty interval (the shaded band)
trend, weekly, yearly, holidays — each component's contribution at every time step

What make_future_dataframe does

Prophet doesn't need lag features or a stationary series — it just needs a column of future dates. make_future_dataframe(periods=365) creates a DataFrame with one row per day: all historical dates plus 365 new ones. You pass this to predict() and Prophet fills in the forecast for every row.

Components Plot — Reading the Decomposition

The components plot is what makes Prophet especially useful for understanding your data. It shows each component separately so you can answer questions that are impossible with a single forecast line.

# uncertainty=False removes the shaded confidence bands from each panel
forecast_model.plot_components(model_predictions, uncertainty=False)
plt.show()
PanelWhat it showsWhat to look for
TrendLong-term growth direction with changepointsWhen did growth accelerate or slow? Any dips?
HolidaysEffect of each named event typeBank holidays → negative (fewer commuters). Strikes → positive (commuters switch to bikes)
Weekly seasonalityAverage pattern across days of the weekWeekdays higher (commuting). Weekends lower.
Yearly seasonalityAverage pattern across months of the yearSummer peak. Winter dip. Clear and expected.
Why the components plot is more valuable than the forecast plot

The forecast line tells you "what will happen." The components tell you "why." You can quantify: summer adds ~8,000 hires per day on average, bank holidays subtract ~12,000, weekdays average 3,000 more than weekends. This kind of insight is impossible to extract from AR or Exponential Smoothing.

Seasonality Parameters — Fourier Order

Prophet uses a Fourier series to model each seasonal component. Think of it as fitting a curve using sums of sine and cosine waves — more waves (higher Fourier order) means the curve can follow more complex, wiggly shapes.

Experimenting with seasonality

# Good fit — smooth but captures the main seasonal pattern
model_good = Prophet(
    holidays=all_holidays_strikes,
    weekly_seasonality=3,
    yearly_seasonality=3,
    daily_seasonality=False
).fit(df_data)

# Underfitting — too smooth, misses real seasonal variation
model_under = Prophet(
    holidays=all_holidays_strikes,
    weekly_seasonality=1,
    yearly_seasonality=1,
    daily_seasonality=False
).fit(df_data)

# Overfitting — follows training noise, unstable forecasts
model_over = Prophet(
    holidays=all_holidays_strikes,
    weekly_seasonality=20,
    yearly_seasonality=20,
    daily_seasonality=False
).fit(df_data)
How to spot underfitting vs overfitting in the seasonal components

Underfitting (order=1): The weekly component is a smooth sine wave — it can't capture that Monday–Friday are all different. The yearly component misses the fine shape of the summer peak.

Good fit (order=3): The weekly component shows distinct values for each weekday and weekend. The yearly component has the right summer/winter shape without being wiggly.

Overfitting (order=20): The components have many sharp spikes and zigzags that don't correspond to any real pattern — the model is fitting noise. Forecasts will look erratic.

Default: Prophet uses order=3 for weekly and order=10 for yearly. These defaults work well for most datasets. Only change them if the components plot shows the seasonal shape is clearly wrong.

Changepoint Detection

A changepoint is a moment in the history where the trend rate changed — growth accelerated, slowed, or reversed. Prophet automatically finds these rather than assuming a single constant growth rate throughout the series.

How Prophet finds changepoints

Prophet places 25 potential changepoints uniformly across the first 80% of the training data. It then uses L1 regularisation (a penalty that pushes small changes to zero) to determine which potential changepoints are real — it uses as few as possible. Changepoints in the last 20% are excluded by default to avoid overfitting the end of the series.

from prophet.plot import add_changepoints_to_plot

# Re-fit with good seasonality parameters
forecast_model = Prophet(
    holidays=all_holidays_strikes,
    weekly_seasonality=3,
    yearly_seasonality=3,
    daily_seasonality=False
)
forecast_model.fit(df_data)
df_dates = forecast_model.make_future_dataframe(periods=365, include_history=True)
model_predictions = forecast_model.predict(df_dates)

# Plot the forecast, then overlay the detected changepoints as vertical lines
fig = forecast_model.plot(model_predictions)
add_changepoints_to_plot(fig.gca(), forecast_model, model_predictions)
plt.show()
How to read the changepoint plot

Vertical dashed lines mark where Prophet detected a shift in trend rate. Compare them against the trend panel in plot_components() — each changepoint corresponds to a visible kink in the trend line. For the TFL bike data you should see a steep positive trend during 2011–2012 (initial adoption period), then a flattening, then possibly a dip around 2013. The changepoints mark exactly when those transitions happened.

Full Workflow Summary

StepCodePurpose
1. Load datapd.read_excel()Get raw time series
2. Rename columns.rename({"Day":"ds", "y_col":"y"})Prophet requires exactly ds and y
3. Build eventsDataFrame with holiday and ds columnsTell model about special days
4. Create modelProphet(holidays=..., weekly_seasonality=3)Configure components
5. Fitmodel.fit(df_data)Estimate all component parameters
6. Future datesmodel.make_future_dataframe(periods=365)Create dates to forecast into
7. Predictmodel.predict(df_dates)Generate yhat + uncertainty intervals
8. Plot forecastmodel.plot(predictions)Visualise forecast vs observed
9. Plot componentsmodel.plot_components(predictions)Understand trend, seasonality, events
10. Changepointsadd_changepoints_to_plot(fig.gca(), model, predictions)See where trend rate shifted

Time Series Analysis

L9: Practical Example

End-to-end time series analysis on real daily pageviews data — full preprocessing pipeline, three models compared, and business insights extracted from Prophet components.

Overview

This lesson walks through a complete real-world time series project from raw data to business insight. The dataset is daily pageviews from a news site covering January 2024 to June 2026.

ItemDetail
DatasetDaily pageviews on the site
PeriodJanuary 2024 – June 2026 (~850 daily observations)
TargetForecast the next 90 days of daily pageviews
Models testedHolt-Winters, Autoregressive (AR), Prophet
Why this dataset is interesting

News site traffic is driven by two competing forces: predictable weekly and yearly patterns (people read more on weekdays, less in December) and completely unpredictable breaking news events that can 2–4× traffic in a single day. This makes it an ideal test for comparing models that can and cannot handle one-off events.

Step 1 — Data Loading and Setup

import pandas as pd
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

pageviews = pd.read_excel(
    "pageviews.xlsx",
    parse_dates=["Day"],
    index_col="Day"
)

# Prophet requires columns named exactly ds and y — rename upfront
df = pageviews.copy()
df = df.reset_index().rename(columns={"Day": "ds", "Page views": "y"})

Events DataFrame — breaking news

Breaking news spikes cannot be explained by trend or seasonality. Prophet has a dedicated events mechanism — pass a DataFrame of known special dates so the model learns their effect separately and keeps them out of the trend estimate.

events = pd.DataFrame({
    "ds": pd.to_datetime([
        "2024-07-13",  # Trump assassination attempt
        "2024-11-05",  # US Election day
        "2025-06-13",  # Air India plane crash - 260+ dead
        "2025-06-22",  # US strikes Iran nuclear sites
        "2026-02-28",  # US & Israel attack Iran - Day 1
        "2026-03-01",  # US & Israel attack Iran - Day 2
    ]),
    "holiday": [
        "trump_assassination_attempt",
        "us_election_2024",
        "air_india_crash",
        "us_iran_nuclear_strike",
        "us_israel_iran_attack",
        "us_israel_iran_attack",  # same name = one effect learned across both days
    ]
})
Why unique event names matter

Each unique name gets its own effect coefficient. If you gave all events the same name, Prophet would average their effects — which is wrong when some events boost traffic and others might suppress it. Separate names give the model freedom to learn that an election adds 20M while a bank holiday subtracts 2M.

Step 2 — Exploratory Data Analysis

Raw time series and weekly average

Why: The first plot tells you what kind of series you're dealing with before choosing any model. You cannot make an informed decision about preprocessing or model selection without seeing the data.

# Raw daily plot
pageviews.plot(figsize=(12, 3))
plt.title("Site pageviews")
plt.show()

# Weekly average — removes daily noise, makes trend and spikes clearer
pageviews.resample("W").mean().plot(figsize=(12, 3))
plt.title("Site pageviews (weekly average)")
plt.show()

What you see: Sharp growth from ~5M to ~20M between January and April 2024. Stable plateau around 18–22M from mid-2024. Clear weekly zigzag throughout. Six visible spikes above 30M.

Day of week pattern

Why: Knowing which days are busiest tells you the shape of weekly seasonality — directly determining seasonal_periods and whether lag-7 AR makes sense.

pageviews["day_of_week"] = pageviews.index.dayofweek
pageviews.groupby("day_of_week")["Page views"].mean().plot(kind="bar")
plt.title("Average pageviews by day of week")
plt.xlabel("Day of week (0=Monday, 6=Sunday)")
plt.show()
pageviews = pageviews.drop(columns=["day_of_week"])

Finding: Only ~11% difference between weekdays and weekends — much flatter than a UK news site. Confirms the traffic is predominantly US-based. Americans read news on weekends almost as much as weekdays, unlike UK audiences with a sharp commuter-driven weekday peak.

Breaking news spike detection

Why: Spikes 2× above normal cannot be explained by trend or seasonality. Left unidentified, they corrupt model estimates — especially Holt-Winters trend. Identifying them upfront lets Prophet absorb them via the events DataFrame.

pageviews[pageviews["Page views"] > 30_000_000]
DatePageviewsEvent
2024-07-1432,122,116Trump assassination attempt — next day surge
2024-11-0641,701,591US Presidential Election results
2025-06-1331,329,220Air India plane crash — 260+ dead
2025-06-2230,195,035US strikes Iran nuclear sites
2026-02-2831,943,786US & Israel attack Iran — Day 1
2026-03-0135,252,983US & Israel attack Iran — Day 2

Step 3 — Preprocessing

Missing dates and values

Why: Time series models assume a regular gapless index. A missing day makes the model treat the surrounding days as consecutive — breaking all lag calculations. NaN values crash most fitting functions.

# Check for date gaps
all_dates = pd.date_range(start=df["ds"].min(), end=df["ds"].max(), freq="D")
print("Missing dates:", len(all_dates.difference(df["ds"])))  # 0

# Check for NaN values
print(df.isna().sum())  # ds: 0, y: 0

Result: No missing dates, no missing values. Data is complete.

Seasonal decomposition

Why: Decomposition separates the series into trend, seasonality, and residuals. This tells you exactly what structure the model needs. If the trend drifts — you need trend handling. If the seasonal panel is consistent — seasonal_periods=7 is reliable. If the residuals have clear spikes — those are your outlier events.

from statsmodels.tsa.seasonal import seasonal_decompose

ts = df.set_index("ds")["y"]
result = seasonal_decompose(ts, model="additive", period=7)
result.plot()
plt.show()
What each panel showed

Trend: Sharp growth Jan–Apr 2024, plateau ~20M from mid-2024, slight decline from late 2025. Breaking news bumps visible.

Seasonal: Clean weekly cycle, amplitude ~1.5M, completely consistent throughout — confirms seasonal_periods=7.

Residuals: Mostly flat near zero with clear spikes matching the identified breaking news events — these are real signals, not noise.

ACF and PACF

Why: ACF tells you whether autocorrelation exists and at which lags — reveals the seasonality structure. PACF strips out indirect chain effects to show only direct dependencies — tells you the AR order. Without these you're guessing model order blindly.

from statsmodels.graphics.tsaplots import plot_acf, plot_pacf

fig, axes = plt.subplots(1, 2, figsize=(16, 4))
plot_acf(ts, lags=30, ax=axes[0])
plot_pacf(ts, lags=30, ax=axes[1])
plt.show()
What the plots showed

ACF: All 30 lags significantly positive, slowly declining from ~0.85 to ~0.45 — classic signature of a strongly autocorrelated series. Expected spike at lag 7 for weekly seasonality was masked by the overall autocorrelation strength.

PACF: Massive spike at lag 1 (~0.9), smaller at lags 2–3, everything else inside the confidence band. Direct dependency is mostly at lag 1 — AR(1) or AR(2) will capture most structure. ar_select_order ultimately confirmed lag 7 as optimal because the same-day-last-week is the most useful predictor for daily news traffic.

Stationarity test (ADF)

Why: AR models require a stationary series — constant mean and variance over time. If non-stationary, differencing is needed before fitting. If already stationary, differencing would hurt by introducing artificial structure.

from statsmodels.tsa.stattools import adfuller

result = adfuller(ts.dropna())
print(f"ADF Statistic: {result[0]:.4f}")  # -4.1013
print(f"p-value: {result[1]:.4f}")        # 0.0009 → STATIONARY
The surprise result

The ACF slow decay looked like a non-stationary trend — but the ADF test said the series IS stationary (p=0.0009). The Jan–Apr 2024 ramp-up was a temporary growth phase, not a permanent trend. Once it stabilised at ~20M it stayed there. ADF detected this correctly.

Important: do NOT apply diff(7) — over-differencing an already-stationary series destroys real signal and makes all three models worse.

Distribution check

Why: Heavily right-skewed distributions benefit from a log transform to stabilise variance. Checking kurtosis tells you how extreme the outliers are.

print(f"Mean:     {ts.mean():,.0f}")   # 17,896,758
print(f"Skewness: {ts.skew():.2f}")   # -0.91
print(f"Kurtosis: {ts.kurtosis():.2f}") # 4.46
StatValueInterpretation
Mean17,896,758Average daily traffic across the full period
Std4,294,71024% of mean — high variation from weekly cycle and breaking news
Skewness-0.91Left skew — early low-traffic ramp-up creates the left tail. No log transform needed (log fixes positive/right skew, not negative)
Kurtosis4.46Heavy tails — election and assassination attempt days are extreme outliers

Conclusion: No transformation needed. Distribution is acceptable for all three models.

Step 4 — Train/Test Split

Why: In-sample predictions always look better than real forecasts — the model has already seen that data. To honestly evaluate forecast accuracy you must test on genuinely unseen future data. Always split chronologically — never randomly.

# Hold out last 90 days — always the END of the series
train = df[df["ds"] < df["ds"].max() - pd.Timedelta(days=90)]
test  = df[df["ds"] >= df["ds"].max() - pd.Timedelta(days=90)]

# Train: 761 rows | Test: 91 rows (Mar–Jun 2026)
⚠️ The test set covers the aftermath of the Feb 2026 Iran attack spike — the hardest forecasting period in the dataset. A model that handles this well genuinely understands the data.

Step 5 — Model 1: Holt-Winters

Why use it: Handles trend and seasonality without requiring stationarity. Fast and interpretable. Works well when the series has a stable seasonal pattern — which this series does (clean weekly cycle).

from statsmodels.tsa.api import ExponentialSmoothing
from sklearn.metrics import mean_absolute_error

train_ts = train.set_index("ds")["y"]
test_ts  = test.set_index("ds")["y"]

tes = ExponentialSmoothing(
    train_ts,
    trend="add",
    seasonal="add",
    seasonal_periods=7
).fit()

tes_forecast = tes.forecast(len(test_ts))
print(f"Holt-Winters MAE: {mean_absolute_error(test_ts, tes_forecast):,.2f}")
# MAE: 15,464,162 — 86% error rate
What went wrong — 86% error rate

The Iran attack days (Feb 28 – Mar 1 2026) caused a massive spike right at the end of training. Holt-Winters saw those final days at 30–35M and concluded the trend is now strongly upward. It has no events mechanism — it cannot distinguish a one-off spike from a genuine trend change. It extrapolated the spike as a new trend and forecast ~36M by June 2026. The actual traffic declined back to ~17M.

Step 6 — Model 2: AR

Why use it: AR models predict the next value from past values. Strong PACF spike at lag 1 confirms this series has exactly the autocorrelation structure AR exploits. ar_select_order automates lag selection via BIC.

from statsmodels.tsa.ar_model import ar_select_order
from statsmodels.api import tsa

train_ts = train.set_index("ds")["y"].asfreq("D")
test_ts  = test.set_index("ds")["y"]

mod = ar_select_order(train_ts, maxlag=14, ic="bic")
optlag = max(mod.ar_lags)  # 7 — same day last week

ar_fit = tsa.AutoReg(train_ts, lags=optlag).fit()
ar_pred = ar_fit.predict(start=len(train_ts), end=len(train_ts)+len(test_ts)-1)

print(f"AR MAE: {mean_absolute_error(test_ts.values, ar_pred.values):,.2f}")
# MAE: 2,642,868 — 15% error rate
Why lag 7 was selected and what it means

BIC selected lag 7 — last week's same-day value — as the optimal predictor. Monday this week is best predicted by last Monday. The weekly pattern is the dominant direct signal in the series. AR(7) correctly identified the direction of the forecast (declining after the Iran attack) but its forecast was too smooth — it couldn't capture the sharp weekly zigzag and decayed the event effect too slowly.

Step 7 — Model 3: Prophet

Why use it: Prophet is designed for exactly this type of data — business time series with multiple seasonalities, trend changepoints, and known one-off events. The events DataFrame tells the model which days are special so breaking news spikes never corrupt the trend estimate.

from prophet import Prophet

model = Prophet(
    holidays=events,
    weekly_seasonality=3,
    yearly_seasonality=10,
    daily_seasonality=False
)
model.fit(df)

future = model.make_future_dataframe(periods=91, include_history=True)
forecast = model.predict(future)

prophet_pred = forecast.set_index("ds")["yhat"].loc[test["ds"].values]
print(f"Prophet MAE: {mean_absolute_error(test['y'].values, prophet_pred.values):,.2f}")
# MAE: 1,674,411 — 9% error rate
Why Prophet won

The events DataFrame correctly neutralised the Iran attack spike — the trend estimate was clean. Prophet correctly started the forecast at ~21M rather than AR's inflated ~29M. Explicit weekly seasonality modelling captured the zigzag AR could only smooth over. No preprocessing pipeline needed at all.

Components plot

model.plot_components(forecast, uncertainty=False)
plt.show()
PanelWhat it showed
TrendRapid growth Jan–Aug 2024 (3M → 18M), plateau, peak Jan 2026, then decline. Breaking news spikes correctly absent — isolated to the holidays panel.
HolidaysTrump attempt: +8.5M. Air India crash: +11M. Iran nuclear strike: +11M. US & Israel attack Iran: +14.5M — the largest single event effect.
WeeklyWednesday peak (+900K). Saturday/Sunday lowest (-1.5M). Classic US mid-week news reading pattern.
YearlyMay strongest (+3M) — US political season. January/November–December weakest (-2.5M). US news cycle drives traffic more than weather.

Final Results and Business Insights

ModelMAEError rateKey weakness
Holt-Winters15,464,16286%Iran spike corrupted trend — forecast went wrong direction
AR2,642,86815%No events mechanism, over-smooth weekly pattern
Prophet1,674,4119%Winner

Business insights from the Prophet components

FindingImplication
Site grew 6× in 8 months in 2024Strong audience acquisition phase — now plateauing
Breaking news adds 8–14M pageviews per eventEvents are the biggest traffic driver — larger than any seasonal effect
US Election added 41M pageviews in one dayPlan infrastructure capacity around major US political events
Mid-week traffic is 2.5M higher than weekendsPublish important content Tuesday–Thursday for maximum reach
May and July are peak monthsPlan editorial capacity around these periods
November–December drops ~3M below baselineHoliday season is consistently weak — reduce publishing targets
Prophet beats AR by 37%Always include known one-off events when forecasting news traffic

Time Series Analysis

L2: Preprocessing for Time Series

Handling missing data, resampling, smoothing, shifting, and making your series stationary.

Why Preprocess Time Series?

Time series data comes with problems that standard ML doesn't face: gaps from sensor failures, irregular sampling, noise masking trends, and non-stationarity. Preprocessing turns raw recorded data into a clean, model-ready signal.

ProblemPreprocessing Fix
Missing observationsForward fill, backward fill, or interpolation
Wrong frequency (too coarse / too fine)Resampling (downsample or upsample)
Noisy signal hiding the trendSmoothing (moving average, EMA)
Need lag features for a modelShifting
Non-stationary (trend / seasonality)Differencing

Handling Missing Data

Time series often have gaps — a sensor goes offline, a market is closed, or data was never recorded. Unlike tabular ML where you drop rows, here every timestamp matters. You must fill the gap to keep the series contiguous.

MethodHow it worksBest for
Forward fill (ffill)Carry the last known value forwardStatus signals (price, category) that don't change until an event
Backward fill (bfill)Pull the next known value backwardWhen future context is available at fill time
Linear interpolationDraw a straight line between neighboursSmoothly changing values (temperature, humidity)
Mean / median imputationFill with the column averageShort random gaps — use sparingly as it flattens variance
 
import pandas as pd

df = pd.read_csv('bikes.csv', parse_dates=['date'], index_col='date')

# Forward fill — carry last value forward
df_ffill = df.fillna(method='ffill')

# Backward fill — pull next value backward
df_bfill = df.fillna(method='bfill')

# Linear interpolation — straight line between neighbours
df_interp = df.interpolate(method='linear')
Which method to choose?

For stock prices or on/off signals, ffill makes sense — nothing changed until the next recorded event. For temperature or sensor readings, linear interpolation is more realistic. Avoid mean imputation for long gaps — it destroys the temporal structure.

Resampling

Resampling changes the frequency of your time series. There are two directions:

DownsamplingUpsampling
DirectionHigh → low frequency (e.g. daily → monthly)Low → high frequency (e.g. daily → 6-hourly)
OperationAggregate many rows into oneCreate new rows between existing ones
Method.resample('M').mean().resample('6h').ffill()
EffectReduces noise, smooths signalInfers values — only as good as the assumption

Downsampling example

# Aggregate daily data to monthly mean
monthly = df['count'].resample('M').mean()

# Weekly mean
weekly = df['humidity'].resample('W').mean()

# Common offset aliases:
# 'D' = day, 'W' = week, 'M' = month end, 'Q' = quarter, 'Y' = year

Upsampling example

# Create 6-hourly rows (0.25 days), then forward-fill the gaps
df_6h = df.resample('6h').ffill()

# Or interpolate linearly instead
df_6h_interp = df.resample('6h').interpolate(method='linear')
⚠️ Upsampling with ffill on bike rental counts is misleading — it implies the same number of bikes were rented every 6 hours. Use domain knowledge to choose the right interpolation method.

Parsing Custom Date Formats

Pandas auto-parses most date strings, but some datasets use non-standard formats (e.g. 2010-Q3 for quarterly data). You must supply a custom parser.

import pandas as pd

# Custom parser for "year-Q{n}" format, e.g. "2009-Q1"
def parse_quarter(s):
    year, q = s.split('-Q')
    month = (int(q) - 1) * 3 + 1   # Q1→Jan, Q2→Apr, Q3→Jul, Q4→Oct
    return pd.Timestamp(year=int(year), month=month, day=1)

df = pd.read_csv(
    'NZAlcoholConsumption.csv',
    index_col=0,
    date_parser=parse_quarter,
    parse_dates=True
)

# Each row is now indexed by a proper Timestamp
# e.g. 2009-01-01, 2009-04-01, 2009-07-01 …
Why does this matter?

Without a datetime index, pandas treats your date column as a plain string. You lose .resample(), .rolling(), and all time-aware plotting. Getting the index right is the foundation of every other preprocessing step.

Smoothing

Raw time series often have high-frequency noise that masks the underlying trend. Smoothing replaces each point with a weighted average of nearby points, revealing the signal beneath.

Moving Average

A sliding window that averages the last k points. All points in the window have equal weight.

# 7-day moving average
df['count_ma7'] = df['count'].rolling(window=7).mean()

# Centre the window (uses points before AND after)
df['count_ma7_c'] = df['count'].rolling(window=7, center=True).mean()

# Rolling std, min, max work the same way
df['count_std7'] = df['count'].rolling(window=7).std()
⚠️ The first window − 1 rows will be NaN because there aren't enough preceding points to fill the window.

Exponential Moving Average (EMA)

Weights decay exponentially — recent observations matter more than old ones. Controlled by span (higher span = smoother, slower to react) or alpha (weight of the newest point, 0–1).

# EMA with span=7 (similar smoothness to a 7-day MA but more weight on recent data)
df['count_ema7'] = df['count'].ewm(span=7).mean()

# Relationship: alpha ≈ 2 / (span + 1)
# span=7 → alpha ≈ 0.25  (25% weight on newest point)
Moving AverageEMA
WeightsEqual across windowExponentially decaying (newest = most)
LagHigh — reacts slowlyLower — reacts faster to changes
NaN at start?Yes, first window−1 rowsNo — uses all available history
Use caseNoise reduction, trend extractionForecasting where recency matters more

Shifting

Shifting moves the entire series forward or backward in time by n steps. This is used to create lag features (what was the value 1 day ago?) or to align two series that have a known time offset.

# Shift forward 1 step — value at row t becomes value at row t+1
df['count_lag1'] = df['count'].shift(1)

# Shift forward 7 steps — last week's count at each row
df['count_lag7'] = df['count'].shift(7)

# Negative shift — peek at future values (use only in analysis, never in models)
df['count_lead1'] = df['count'].shift(-1)

# Shifting creates NaN at the boundary:
# shift(1) → first row becomes NaN
# shift(-1) → last row becomes NaN
Creating lag features for models

Many time series models (and even standard ML models applied to time series) need lag features — the value from k steps back as an input variable. shift(k) creates these features. Drop the resulting NaN rows before training.

Stationarity and Differencing

Most time series models assume the series is stationary — constant mean and variance with no trend or seasonality. If the ADF test shows non-stationarity (p ≥ 0.05), you need to transform the data.

Differencing

The most common fix: subtract the value from the previous step. This removes a linear trend in one pass.

# First-order differencing: removes a linear trend
df['count_diff1'] = df['count'].diff(1)

# Seasonal differencing (lag = season length): removes seasonality
# e.g. for weekly seasonality in daily data, lag = 7
df['count_diff7'] = df['count'].diff(7)

# Second-order differencing: removes a quadratic trend
df['count_diff2'] = df['count'].diff(1).diff(1)

# Check if it worked — ADF test after differencing
from statsmodels.tsa.stattools import adfuller
result = adfuller(df['count_diff1'].dropna())
print(f'p-value: {result[1]:.4f}')  # want p < 0.05
ProblemDifferencing lagWhat it removes
Linear trenddiff(1)Steady upward / downward drift
Weekly seasonality (daily data)diff(7)Day-of-week pattern
Annual seasonality (monthly data)diff(12)Month-of-year pattern
Quadratic trenddiff(1).diff(1)Accelerating growth / decay
How many times to difference?

Start with diff(1) and re-run the ADF test. If still non-stationary, difference again. Most real-world series become stationary after 1–2 rounds. Over-differencing introduces unnecessary noise — stop once the ADF test passes.

Preprocessing Pipeline Summary

A typical time series preprocessing workflow follows this order:

# 1. Load with datetime index
df = pd.read_csv('data.csv', parse_dates=['date'], index_col='date')

# 2. Handle missing values
df = df.interpolate(method='linear')

# 3. Resample to desired frequency (optional)
df = df.resample('D').mean()

# 4. Smooth if needed (optional)
df['count_smooth'] = df['count'].rolling(7).mean()

# 5. Create lag features for modelling
df['count_lag1'] = df['count'].shift(1)

# 6. Make stationary if required by the model
df['count_diff'] = df['count'].diff(1)

# 7. Drop NaN rows created by shifting / differencing
df = df.dropna()
Techniquepandas functionKey parameter
Forward fillfillna(method='ffill')—
Interpolationinterpolate(method='linear')method: linear, polynomial, spline
Downsamplingresample('M').mean()offset alias: D, W, M, Q, Y
Upsamplingresample('6h').ffill()fill method after resample
Moving averagerolling(7).mean()window size
EMAewm(span=7).mean()span or alpha
